Get Stronger With These 5 Movements
2026-09-15

Get Stronger With These 5 Movements

TIME | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings You don’t have to be a bodybuilder to recognize the importance of building muscle. Strength training is linked to stronger bones, healthier joints, less pain, and a lengthier lifespan. Generally speaking, “getting stronger does mean that we live longer,” says Charlie Grundas, a certified personal trainer and owner of We, The Collective Fitness in Chicago. It helps you improve in any athletic endeavors, and it also keeps you moving fluidly throughout the activities of your daily life, like climbing stairs and carrying groceries. But if you’re not used to strength training, where do you start? Grundas recommends focusing on just five types of movements: squat, hinge, push, pull, and carry, which are considered the foundational movements of a full-body strength-training routine. “I find those to be very human movements,” he says. You can mix and match these movements into two full-body strengthening workouts per week. As they start to feel easier over several weeks, you can gradually increase how many exercises you do or how much weight you use. Below are some go-to strength-training exercises he and other experts recommend—plus, how to do each one. Squats of all kinds help you maintain the ability to stand up from sitting, Grundas says. This single-leg version, also called a Bulgarian split squat, helps build strength and stability in one leg at a time. That’s helpful for your daily life, because walking, running, and climbing stairs are essentially single-leg activities, says Will Hewitt, certified personal trainer and director of training at Flight House Fitness in Charlestown, Massachusetts. Start standing on one leg, propping the toes of your other foot up on a bench or step behind you. You can do this with just your body weight or hold a dumbbell in each hand. Keeping your weight in your standing foot, bend that knee to lower your body until your back knee is just above the floor. Drive through your front foot to return to the starting position. Complete all repetitions—aim for 8 to 15, depending on your fitness level and goals—then switch sides. If you have trouble balancing in that position, keep both feet on the floor instead, Hewitt says. Start in a staggered stance and lower your body until both knees are bent to 90 degrees. Complete all your reps before switching sides. A deadlift is primarily a lower-body movement. But when you add weight, you’ll also work your forearms, upper back, and grip strength, Hewitt says. Start standing with your feet hip-width apart and a slight bend in your knees, holding a dumbbell in each hand or a loaded barbell in both hands in front of your thighs. Keeping that slight bend in your knees, push your hips back and lower the weight in front of your legs until you feel a stretch in the backs of your thighs. Pause briefly, then drive through your feet to return to the starting position. Complete all repetitions, aiming for 8 to 15. If you’re new to this movement, start with a single kettlebell in front of you rather than a barbell, Hewitt says. “That feels way more comfortable if you’ve never done one before, and eliminates some of the lower-back apprehension that people might have,” he says. Not only does a push-up work your shoulders and chest, but it’s also good for your core. “A push-up is essentially a moving plank,” Hewitt says. Start balancing on your hands and the balls of your feet, hands under your shoulders, legs straight behind you. Bend your elbows to lower your chest toward the floor as low as you can comfortably go. Drive through your hands to return to the starting position. Place your hands on an elevated surface for an incline push-up. This can even be a wall if you’re new to push-ups. As you get stronger, keep finding lower surfaces, like a counter, a desk, a couch, or a few large books on the floor, and eventually you’ll be ready for a full push-up. Even when modified, pull-ups test shoulder mobility, upper-body strength, and grip strength, Hewitt says. Pull-ups also help develop back strength, which powers upper-body movements and improves stability, Grundas says. But plenty of people neglect their back muscles in favor of “show” muscles like biceps and abs that they see in the mirror, he says. Start hanging from a pull-up bar with your palms facing away from you about shoulder-distance apart. Keeping your shoulders back and down, pull yourself up until your chin is above the bar. Slowly lower back down to return to the starting position. You can use resistance bands to reduce some of your body weight for assisted pull-ups. You can also try a chin-up, which is a similar motion but with your palms facing toward you on the bar. “When we go grocery shopping, how many trips should it take to take all the groceries in?” Grundas says. If you want the answer to be “one,” this type of move will help you become strong enough to get there. “Grip strength is a great indicator of [overall] strength,” he says. In fact, research suggests that older adults with weaker grip strength show accelerated signs of aging and have a higher risk of heart disease and death. Start standing with your feet hip-width apart, holding a dumbbell or kettlebell in each hand at your sides. Keeping your spine long and your shoulders down and back, walk forward for a predetermined distance, such as 20 yards, or a set time, such as 30 seconds. Use heavier or lighter weights as needed. For an additional balance challenge, hold one heavy weight on one side, and make sure you don’t lean to that side while you walk. Generally, adults should try to do at least two full-body strength-training workouts each week. You can start by doing 12 to 20 repetitions per exercise, one to three times, with up to 90 seconds of rest between sets, according to the National Academy of Sports Medicine. But if you can only do eight or fewer reps at first, start where you are and build up gradually from there. As you gain experience, you might tailor your reps and sets to your goals, like building more muscle size or endurance. But you don’t have to figure this all out on your own. Certified personal trainers and certified strength and conditioning specialists are trained to identify the best exercises for you, depending on your goals, ability, and preferences. “It’s totally normal to feel a little lost,” Hewitt says. A professional can also coach you through the best modifications for you. “We're going to do it in a way that works for you and wherever you're at,” he says.

Here’s What the Science Really Says About the Health and Climate Risks of Carbon Pollution

Here’s What the Science Really Says About the Health and Climate Risks of Carbon Pollution

Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The Trump Administration on Monday repealed carbon pollution rules for coal- and gas-powered plants. And at the heart of the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA) explanation for its move is the long-debunked idea that, as the agency claims, “emissions from power plants have no material impact on global climate change” and that “the targeted emissions are global in nature, and any potential public health harms are too uncertain, conjectural, remote, and convoluted to tie specifically to the U.S. power sector.” The decision goes against decades of science proving that cutting pollution has myriad benefits for human and environmental health. EPA Administrator Lee Zeldin announced the move at the G20 Energy Abundance Ministerial in Houston, Texas, on Sept. 14. The EPA will now hold a public hearing for the proposal over a 15 day period, and have a 45-day public comment period. The U.S. power sector is a major source of greenhouse gas emissions. According to the most recent data, in 2024, the U.S. power sector emitted 1,427 million metric tons of carbon dioxide, the second largest U.S. source by sector. A report published by the Institute for Policy Integrity at the New York University School of Law in May 2025 found that if the U.S. power sector were its own country, it would be the sixth-largest emitter in the world. The move comes at a time when the United Nations warns that the world is set to overshoot the 1.5°C goal to limit warming—and that the world will reach 1.8°C of warming unless immediate changes are made to reduce global emissions and remove carbon from the atmosphere. The EPA’s repeal concerns climate pollution standards originally finalized by the Biden Administration in 2024. The regulations applied to coal-burning plants in operation and any prospective gas-burning plants, and required coal plants to install technology to capture most of its carbon emissions by 2039 in order to keep operating, while gas plants would have been required to operate on cleaner fuels, like hydrogen. The Biden Administration predicted that the rule would have prevented up to 1,200 premature deaths, 360,000 cases of asthma symptoms, 48,000 school absence days, and 57,000 lost work days in 2035 alone. It also projected up to $370 billion in climate and public health net benefits over the next two decades. The repeal of this rule is the latest in the Trump Administration’s attempts to roll back federal climate regulation. In February, the EPA finalized its repeal of the endangerment finding, the landmark 2009 ruling that provided a legal underpinning for greenhouse gas regulation. The agency also ruled that the Clean Air Act does not authorize the EPA to regulate vehicle tailpipe emissions, and eased Biden-era restrictions on hydrofluorocarbons, polluting chemicals used in refrigerators and air-conditioning. The Trump Administration has also made a number of moves to keep polluting coal plants alive—even though coal is among the most costly power sources and the air pollution it generates has been linked to a number of adverse health effects, including increased risk of asthma and cancer. In February, the ​​Energy Department ordered several coal-burning plants that had been slated for retirement to stay open. Also that month, Trump loosened Biden-era restrictions on coal burning plants, known as Mercury and Air Toxics Standards for power plants, and ordered the Pentagon to purchase more coal-based electricity. The Department of Energy was also directed to allocate $175 million in funding for six projects to upgrade coal plants in four states. While the EPA under the Trump Administration says that the health impacts are “uncertain,” experts say that scientific evidence overwhelmingly shows the opposite to be true. The Trump Administration’s own analysis from 2025 found that eliminating the carbon pollution standards for coal and gas powered plants would result in thousands of premature deaths and tens of billions of dollars in increased health costs for Americans. “The science is very clear,” says Jim Zhang, professor of global and environmental health at Duke University. “It all shows that these pollutants cause cardiovascular disease, respiratory disease, exacerbates asthma, exacerbates heart disease, and are associated with increased deaths and emergency room visits.” Communities located around power plants, which are disproportionately lower income, are exposed to a great risk of experiencing a number of health impacts, including asthma, cancer, and heart and lung disease. Exposure to fine particulate air pollutants from coal-fired power plants (known as coal PM2.5) is associated with a risk of mortality more than double that of exposure to PM2.5 from other sources. “If you take away the regulation, then that pollution is going to happen, and these health effects are going to happen,” says Meredith Hankins, federal climate legal director at the Natural Resources Defense Council. Worsening carbon dioxide emissions also exacerbates climate change—heating the planet and worsening extreme weather events, wildfires, coastal erosion, flooding, hurricanes, all of which come with health impacts. Extreme weather events cause injuries and deaths, disrupt access to medical care, and impact mental health. They can also indirectly impact access to clean water or healthy food. While the U.S. is repealing these standards, the health impacts will transcend borders. “The health damage is not at local level but global level,” says Zhang. The environmental and health impacts will be felt—despite what the EPA might say. “It is certainly possible to have policies that assume climate change will have no impacts, and that is what we are doing,” says Michael Greenstone, director of the Institute for Climate and Sustainable Growth at the University of Chicago. “But the atmosphere and the climate don't recognize those misstatements, and we will be stuck with a nastier, more virulent form of climate change.”

How to Tell When Caregiver Stress Is Harming Your Health
2026-09-15

How to Tell When Caregiver Stress Is Harming Your Health

TIME | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Sue Ryan had been her husband’s sole caregiver for just under five years before her facade of keeping it all together first began to crack. It was a long time coming. She’d spent countless nights up late correcting mistakes in the household finances—a task her husband had handled before Alzheimer’s disease made it difficult. She no longer went to the gym, or on walks, or to her own medical appointments. She waved away every offer of help with some version of No, no, I’ve got this. So when Ryan watched the potato chip fall from her husband’s lunch plate, she felt a flash of anger: I’ve been doing all these things for you. You can’t even keep a potato chip on your plate? She didn’t say it. She gave him more chips, told him, “Honey, I’ll be right back,” and retreated to the bathroom, where she sat down and began to shake. “Oh my gracious,” she remembers thinking. “What just happened?” Ryan, who has spent 40 years caring for various family members and co-founded the caregiver-support organization The Caregiver’s Journey, had been sacrificing pieces of her own life without tallying the toll. “I didn’t realize the impact of giving up a little bit here, shaving this off, shaving that off,” she says. Caregiver stress can have serious consequences for physical and mental health. It’s associated with anxiety, depression, sleep problems, mood disorders, and burnout, says Dr. Aditi Nerurkar, a stress expert and lecturer in the division of global health and social medicine at Harvard Medical School. But how can you tell when caregiving is starting to take a toll on your health—and what can you do? The clearest warning sign isn’t one terrible day or week—it’s a pattern. Are you regularly struggling to fall asleep, barely eating (or wolfing down five doughnuts a day), snapping at people you love, or unable to focus on the novels you once loved? Maybe you’ve stopped calling friends because you don’t have the bandwidth for chit-chat. What matters is whether these changes have become “consistent and persistent,” says Diane Mariani, a licensed clinical social worker and director of Caring for Caregivers at Rush University Medical Center. Your friends and family members might notice them first. If they tell you that you don’t seem like yourself, resist the automatic “I’m fine” and seriously consider what they’re seeing. Poor sleep deserves particular attention. Even when Ryan slept, she says, she didn’t truly rest. It felt like she always had “one eye half open,” waiting for the next problem. “There’s a layer of stress even when you think, ‘I’ve got this,’” she says. “You’re waiting for the other shoe to drop.” Caregivers often postpone their own screenings, brush off symptoms they would normally get checked out, or let chronic conditions go unmanaged. “You keep pushing things out,” Mariani says. Perhaps you cancel a mammogram because your loved one is in the hospital, or you don’t have anyone who can stay with them while you’re away. Next thing you know, you’re a year overdue. Caregiving also disrupts the habits that help people stay healthy. “What we hear a lot from caregivers is the change in their sleeping, eating, and even exercise,” Mariani says. Over time, poor sleep and chronic stress can affect blood pressure and immune function, she adds, while missed appointments make it harder to catch new problems or manage existing ones. That doesn’t mean every headache, stomachache, or other symptom is caused by caregiving stress. New, persistent, or concerning symptoms need medical evaluation. Mariani and her colleagues routinely ask caregivers whether they have a primary-care doctor and when they last saw them. If you can’t remember the answer—or you’ve repeatedly canceled your own care—that’s a warning sign. As Nerurkar puts it, caregivers often land “at the bottom of that to-do list—and often they’re not even on the list.” Caregivers often de-prioritize their own health because they believe that’s what a strong, loving person does. If the situation feels unbearable, they might decide they simply need to become more resilient. Nerurkar calls this “toxic resilience”: a mind-over-matter approach built around “productivity at all costs, ‘all systems go’ all the time.” Real resilience accounts for human limits and the brain’s need for rest and recovery. “It is a myth, categorically untrue, that people who are resilient cannot get burned out,” she says. “They can and they do.” Guilt can reinforce the same pattern. A spouse may have promised years ago never to move their partner into a care facility. An adult child might believe they’re responsible for managing everything alone. In some families and cultures, that responsibility traditionally falls to one person, often the oldest daughter. Ryan calls some of these commitments “promises you’re not meant to keep.” They’re made out of love, before anyone knows what a serious illness will eventually require. “It becomes, ‘I should take care of him. I promised him I would do this. I’m not going to go back on a promise,’” she says. Ryan once made a promise like that. But she ultimately moved her husband into a care community because his dementia had progressed, and he needed more social interaction than she could provide at home. It was, she says, “the wisest choice for his lifestyle, and his safety and mine.” Mariani suspects that most caregivers can already tell when a situation has become unsustainable. “I think most caregivers do kind of know,” she says. “They just don’t know the solution.” Ask yourself: “If nothing changes, can I continue living this way for another three or six months?” Then identify which part is wearing you down most. Is it getting up four times a night? Managing medications you don’t understand? Never being able to leave the house? Handling every appointment while your siblings offer advice from several states away? “I took this on. I want to do this. This is meaningful for me,” caregivers often tell Mariani. “I just don’t know if I can sustain it.” That’s why she avoids lecturing people about “self-care.” To someone who’s already overwhelmed, it can sound like “another task for their long list,” she says—or another reminder that they’re still not doing enough. Instead, Mariani asks: “What’s something you would like to have time for?” Maybe you miss your morning walk or reading before bed. Start there, and figure out what would have to change to bring it back. If you can’t walk because your loved one can’t be left alone, ask someone to stay with them for 30 minutes. If you keep missing your own appointment because you’re responsible for your parent’s transportation, ask a relative to drive them. Try: “I want to keep doing this, but I can’t sustain it exactly as it is. Could you handle the grocery shopping for the next few weeks?” Or: “Could you stay with Mom on Tuesday so I can make it to my appointment?” But it’s easier said than done, and our society doesn’t currently provide enough support for caregivers, experts argue. Before the potato-chip incident, Ryan routinely—and politely—turned down friends who offered to lend a hand. “I can lie to myself, and I can believe it,” she says. Her best friend, Lynn, was less convinced. After Ryan told her what had happened, she asked Lynn to become an accountability partner—a person who could tell her when she was taking on more than she could handle. Ryan also wrote a list of what someone could do if they had five minutes, 10 minutes, half an hour, two hours, half a day, or overnight. Then she contacted people who had previously offered to help and asked what they would be willing to take on. The tasks don’t have to involve hands-on care. Someone could pick up groceries, schedule appointments, call the insurance company, bring over dinner, research respite-care programs, or sit with your loved one while you leave the house. If you’re supporting a caregiver, offer something specific: “I’m going to the grocery store tomorrow—send me your list.” “I can take Dad to his appointment Thursday.” “I’ll stay with Mom while you get your mammogram.” If the person you want to help resists, Mariani suggests telling them: “I really care about you, and it would mean a lot to me if I could do something for you.” Then offer a task instead of asking them to invent one. One thing to avoid: dispensing advice from the sidelines. Caregivers often hear some version of “Why don’t you…” or “Have you tried…” from people who don’t understand their daily reality. “You don’t know, because you’re not here,” Mariani says. Start by listening. If you’re dealing with ongoing changes to your sleep, mood, appetite, relationships, or overall health, contact your primary-care doctor and let them know you’re a caregiver. Don’t dismiss new physical symptoms as “just stress,” or assume a worsening health problem will improve once life settles down. Ask the health system treating your loved one whether it offers caregiver specialists, social workers, counseling, or support groups. Local and condition-specific organizations may also provide respite care, meal delivery, transportation, or in-home assistance. Support groups can be particularly useful because “everybody gets it,” Mariani says. Caregivers can trade practical advice without having to explain why an outsider-supplied solution won’t work. If researching resources feels like one responsibility too many, hand that job to someone who keeps asking how they can help: “Find out what caregiver support is available through Mom’s hospital, and send me the three best options.” Accepting help ultimately benefited both Ryan and her husband. He was highly social, and when friends stopped by, he got more company and stimulation. Ryan, meanwhile, stopped reflexively turning everyone away. “I went from, No, I got this, to yes,” she says. “People wanted to lean in. They wanted to help. I got out of my own way.”

America’s Investing Identity Crisis
2026-09-15

America’s Investing Identity Crisis

TIME | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings What compelled hundreds of thousands of people around the world to invest in the initial public offering of Elon Musk’s SpaceX this summer? It certainly wasn’t the company’s financial metrics. Despite the success of its core rocket business, the company is burning through a prodigious amount of money. Yet SpaceX still managed to pull off a record-breaking IPO, transforming Musk into the world’s first trillionaire. Although an outlier, the SpaceX IPO is not unique. It is merely an extreme manifestation of two remarkable trends that have become increasingly hard to ignore in financial markets: ordinary investors are a huge and growing force, and their feelings can often trump fundamentals. This is becoming apparent around the world, but nowhere more so than in the U.S., where ordinary people have embraced speculation both as a way to get rich and as a reflection of their own beliefs with perhaps greater zeal than anyone else on the planet. The first point is easy to quantify. Ordinary retail investors now account for over 20% of U.S. stock market activity, according to estimates by Jefferies, an investment bank. That is roughly twice the level of a decade ago and means that they now account for almost as much trading volume as mutual funds, hedge funds, and banks combined. The impact on markets is apparent everywhere. The latter point is harder to prove with hard numbers, but the anecdotal evidence is heaping up. To the consternation of many traditional investors, what securities you buy have become almost like an extension of one’s identity—whether that is the stock of a dowdy utility or racy computer games maker, cryptocurrencies, or even dabbling in the newfangled prediction markets. Finance has long shaped America. But today, I would argue, this shape has become deformed. When did investing stop being merely financial and become an expression of identity? Why do individual Americans see investing as a chance to prove who they are and what they are worth? And what does this pressure say about who America is as a country, collectively? The roots of this uniquely American phenomenon of conjoined identity and rampant speculation can arguably be traced back over a century to the titanic bond-sale program that financed the U.S. entry into the First World War. When the U.S. formally declared war on Germany in 1917, the task of figuring out how to pay for the army, navy, and weapons America needed fell to President Woodrow Wilson’s son-in-law, Treasury Secretary William McAdoo. McAdoo was an athletic, photogenic, and decisive former lawyer and streetcar executive. He fizzed with energy and was as progressive in his business policies as he was regressive in his racial politics (McAdoo advocated forcefully for gender equality and what today we would call “stakeholder capitalism”, but introduced segregation at the Treasury, and was later endorsed by the Ku Klux Klan when he unsuccessfully ran for president). McAdoo knew that taxes alone couldn’t shoulder the entire burden of the war, given the scale of the money needed. The problem was that the U.S. government bond market had by then shriveled up. The size totaled under $1 billion, and most of it was stuffed away in bank vaults rather than traded. Moreover, most ordinary Americans were oblivious to what a bond even was. Wall Street bankers pointed out to McAdoo that there were probably only about 350,000 bondholders in a country of about 100 million inhabitants at the time. They therefore recommended that the Treasury Secretary limit his first bond sale to $1 billion, and target established investors with a juicy interest rate of at least 5 percent. Even this, they felt, would be ambitious. Yet McAdoo balked at their pessimism. “It was true that [Americans] know little or nothing about government bonds, but we would tell them,” he wrote in his memoirs. To thrill the public, he decided to dub the bonds “Liberty Loans,” and set up a sprawling War Loan Organization to mass-market them. It proved a stunning success. Wherever you went in the U.S. those days, you would gaze at a billboard, stumble over an exhibition, be handed a leaflet, enjoy a cake sale, be accosted at work, or get dragged into a march that were all designed to tout Liberty Loans. When bankers initially struggled to communicate with the masses, celebrities like Charlie Chaplin were enlisted to burnish the pitch. Edward Bernays—often considered the father of modern public relations and the author of a pioneering book on propaganda in the 1920s—orchestrated sophisticated plays on patriotism, sentiment, or personal political yearnings to entice investors who might have been put off by the paltry interest rates on offer. Often, the push to subscribe to a Liberty bond sale crossed over into crass shaming. Sometimes, so-called “dollar slackers” were even confronted with naked intimidation from groups who pressured people to buy bonds. The Atlantic excoriated this as “borrowing with a club”, and warned that “mob rule by the rich, with the able assistance of hoodlums” could stir support for socialists. Even future president Warren Harding complained that the selling drives were “hysterical and unseemly”. Nonetheless, Liberty Loans were genuinely popular. Buying them became a communal expression of belonging that crossed social, racial, and religious lines in a divided, polyglot nation. In fact, the War Loan Organization deliberately stoked competition among different groups to see who would demonstrate the greatest fealty to America—as evidenced by the volume of their bond purchases. This was particularly important for many women and Black Americans, who hoped that herculean efforts to help support the war might further their aspirations for equal treatment. All told, the Treasury sold five low-cost mammoth Liberty Loans to finance the war, which raised an astonishing $21.4 billion. Relative to the size of the U.S. economy then and now, this is the equivalent of selling approximately $9 trillion of bonds today. Thus, an unprecedented feat of financial salesmanship transformed a war that was initially not overwhelmingly popular into a unifying national endeavor. Unfortunately, this unity quickly dissolved when the war was over, with America wracked by racial unrest, labor strife, and social tensions. Women received the right to vote in 1920, but the wartime efforts of Black Americans and other minorities were mostly ignored. McAdoo’s later career was blighted by financial scandal and two unsuccessful Ku Klux Klan-supported tilts for the presidency. Nonetheless, his Liberty Loans proved transformative for the fabric of America’s financial system. It is hard to overstate just how impactful the bond sales were for the country’s culture of investment. Remember, when the Treasury Secretary first asked bankers for an appraisal of the Treasury market’s health at the start of the war, they had estimated that only 350,000 Americans owned bonds. By the end of the conflict, about 34 million Americans had purchased some form of federal bond—over a third of the country at the time. This spilled over into the stock market as well. Researchers have later studied the long-run county-level data on how American households save money and found that areas with greater Liberty bond program participation were far more likely to invest in bonds and stocks in the future as well. In fact, a paper published by the National Bureau of Economic Research last year estimated that 20% fewer Americans would have held stocks had the Liberty Loan campaigns not been conducted. The legacy has long been apparent in both the unusual scale and nature of how Americans invest. But social media and gamified trading apps are now obliterating the always-blurry lines between investing, speculation, and pure gambling. As Warren Buffett observed in 2023: “Markets now exhibit far more casino-like behavior than they did when I was young. The casino now resides in many homes and daily tempts the occupants.” And leaders like JPMorgan Chase CEO Jamie Dimon have directly likened increasingly popular prediction markets to gambling—while caveating that “people have been gambling forever.” Political polarization is now introducing a new and arguably dangerous element to the phenomenon. The cohesion fostered by the Liberty Loan program and its feelings-first marketing has been distorted into yet another force that is driving Americans apart. Which brings us back to SpaceX. For some investors, SpaceX is an overhyped and overpriced monstrosity, run by a controversial and mercurial founder. For others, betting on SpaceX is an expression of faith. They believe in Musk and his visions of spacebound data centers, thinking machines, robots that can mine Mars, and ultimately, interplanetary travel. To them, today’s stock price is a humdrum secondary concern. This is more widespread than you might think. Even members of Congress have been found to signal their beliefs through their investment activities. “For U.S. legislators, the stock market functions not only as a venue for wealth accumulation but also as a stage for identity signaling,” a paper published earlier this year noted. But you need not be a finance professor to see that this is a dangerous trend. Markets work best when decisions are based on fundamentals, not feelings. And by reflecting America’s political polarization, identity investing can also accentuate it. Unfortunately, as the Liberty Loan story indicates, the reality may be that the American zeal for speculation and a willingness to sometimes let emotions trump rationality may always have been intertwined. The extent may wax and wane according to the market cycles, but by now they might be impossible to separate. Luana Lopes Lara Is on the Frontlines of Evolving Prediction Markets
2026-09-15

Luana Lopes Lara Is on the Frontlines of Evolving Prediction Markets

TIME | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Luana Lopes Lara is inherently an optimist—perhaps because Brazilians, she says, have a more positive outlook than people from other countries. That trait has served her well as chief operating officer of Kalshi, the New York-based prediction market that has boomed in recent years as people take up placing money on everything from what words the President will use in his speeches to the price of a Costco hot dog. “I probably wouldn’t be here if I hadn’t had this Brazilian culture and background,” says Lopes Lara, 30. “I’m very optimistic and positive, but it’s not a blind optimism—it’s more like I believe that in the end, things will work out.” She still leans on some Brazilian traditions, like always trying to get out of bed on her right foot and then taking a few hops on it, eating lentils for New Year’s, and jumping over seven waves in the ocean for good luck. Lopes Lara put her optimism to work when the company decided to sue Kalshi’s regulator, the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission, arguing that the company should be allowed to list political event contracts. Kalshi won the case in September 2024, which allowed the platform to feature the 2024 elections, with users favoring Donald Trump over Kamala Harris when polls were forecasting an even race. Since then, the company’s valuation has grown to $22 billion, and in December 2025, Lopes Lara unseated 31-year-old Scale cofounder Lucy Guo to become the world’s youngest self-made woman billionaire at 29, according to Forbes. Lopes Lara’s parents, a math teacher and an electrical engineer, pushed their two daughters to be financially independent, so they would never need to rely on partners for money. But her parents’ vision of success included going to a public university in Brazil and getting a job as a doctor, engineer, or lawyer, she says. She wanted more for herself: “I always wanted to do bigger things.” Lopes Lara was accepted into the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Harvard, Yale, and Stanford while she was performing as a ballet dancer in Austria, having trained at the only official affiliate school of the Russian Bolshoi Theater outside of Moscow. She chose MIT, she says, because people there seemed more passionate about what they were pursuing than students did at Harvard. It was at MIT that Lopes Lara got to know her Kalshi co-founder, Tarek Mansour, when he sat next to her in the first row in an algorithms class so he could learn from her, she says. These days, Mansour handles many public-facing tasks as CEO and Lopes Lara handles internal operations. The role has forced her to unlearn some of the perfectionist tendencies she honed for so long as a ballerina, she says, where tilting your finger a centimeter in the wrong direction can ruin the aesthetics. “For a startup, you need the opposite,” she says. “You need to move very fast.” The speed at which Kalshi is moving has caught the eye of regulators outside the federal government, resulting in court challenges in states including New York and Washington. So far, its federal regulator has remained steadfast in holding that Kalshi can legally operate. Lopes Lara says she believes Kalshi’s case will prevail—after all, it was a President Biden-appointed judge who first gave the company the green light to list elections, and it’s the Trump Administration that continues to support the company. “At the end of the day, it’s because we’re right on the law,” she says. Many state attorneys general disagree, making her position, unquestionably, what an optimist might say.

Trump’s Position on AI Makes No Sense
2026-09-15

Trump’s Position on AI Makes No Sense

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
The AI panic has arrived. Tech CEOs are practically begging the government to make them slow down. But Donald Trump isn’t buying any of it. On Sunday, he dismissed calls for AI regulation on the grounds that doing so would surrender America’s position in the global AI arms race. “Look, we’re leading China in AI,” the president told reporters. “We’re the most sophisticated country in the world. And frankly, I want to keep it that way, because whoever wins AI wins.” He repeated the sentiment less decorously in a Truth Social post yesterday describing the calls for regulation as “a SICK conspiracy going on against AI,” adding that “the only one that is happy about it is China.” But if Trump is truly concerned about staying ahead of China in the AI race, he hasn’t been acting like it. Late last year, his administration lifted export controls on advanced chips made by the American company Nvidia. These chips are the most crucial input to AI development. The policy is the equivalent of declaring that the United States must remain ahead of Russia in the nuclear-arms race, while allowing American firms to sell uranium to the Kremlin. Indeed, America’s monopoly on advanced computer chips might be the biggest reason the U.S. has kept ahead of Chinese AI companies so far. Experts generally agree that China is level with or ahead of America when it comes to engineering talent, training data, and energy supply. But training a top AI model also requires highly advanced computer chips capable of performing trillions of calculations a second. Nvidia is the only company in the world that can produce such chips at scale. Read: What really happens if China wins the AI race? That’s why, in October 2022, the Biden administration announced that it would ban Nvidia from selling its most advanced semiconductors to China. The aim of the policy was to slow China’s AI development by withholding the one resource that it did not have. When Trump took office in January 2025, the policy seemed to be working. Experts estimated that the leading American AI models were well ahead of the best Chinese models, and Chinese AI companies were citing export controls as one of the biggest obstacles to their growth. “Money has never been the problem for us,” Liang Wenfeng, the CEO of the Chinese AI company DeepSeek, told a Chinese media outlet in 2024. “Bans on shipments of advanced chips are the problem.” But over the course of 2025, Trump began loosening the restrictions. In May, the administration halted the implementation of a Biden-era rule meant to prevent Nvidia chips from being smuggled into China via third-party countries. Then, in July, it lifted controls on the sale of Nvidia’s H20 chip. Finally, in December, the White House went even further by removing the ban on the far more powerful H200, Nvidia’s second-best chip, which is estimated to be nearly six times more powerful than any chip China had direct access to previously. Experts immediately warned that lifting the ban was a bad idea. The Institute for Progress, a think tank focused on science and technology policy, estimated that the U.S. would have had access to roughly 20 to 50 times more compute power than China by 2026 if it had kept full chip restrictions in place; unrestricted H200 exports could shrink that gap to almost nothing. “Compute power is the only reason we’ve retained our advantage so far,” Saif Khan, who co-authored that report, told me at the time. “And we’re about to just give it away.” Chinese companies have indeed begun to catch up since the controls were lifted. Over the first half of this year, multiple Chinese AI firms released models that perform nearly as highly as Anthropic and OpenAI’s top models, most of them at a fraction of the price. Experts now believe that the AI gap between the U.S. and China is about six months and closing fast. Rogé Karma: Data centers are a distraction Some of this progress seems to be the result of Chinese firms taking advantage of the Trump administration’s loose policies. The most advanced Chinese model to date, Kimi-K3, appears to have been trained by remotely accessing a cluster of Nvidia’s most advanced chips via a data center in Thailand—a loophole that was made possible when Trump rolled back restrictions on chip sales to third-party countries. An analysis by Epoch AI, an AI-focused think tank, found that up to 59 percent of China’s total AI-chip stockpile at the end of 2025 had been smuggled into the country. Now that China has legal access to Nvidia’s second-best chip, however, Chinese companies won’t need to rely on whatever compute power they can scrape together through the black market. They can simply buy the chips that they need. A few months ago, Liang, the CEO of DeepSeek, lamented that his company had “relatively little” access to chips last year, but that “this year we’re very aggressively expanding this compute” with chips that are “basically all Nvidia.” All of which means that the next generation of Chinese models will likely be even more powerful. Trump is right that a unilateral AI slowdown would create a serious risk of the U.S. being overtaken by China. But the solution is not a perpetual anything-goes AI arms race. Instead, the U.S. should pursue a nonproliferation agreement with China in which both sides agree to a mutual deceleration of AI progress. Trump wants to use the threat of Chinese AI development as an excuse to keep the U.S. industry accelerating—even as his own policies make that threat worse.

What Trump’s Bizarre Posts Are Teaching Us About AI
2026-09-15

What Trump’s Bizarre Posts Are Teaching Us About AI

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Here is a selective list of the dozens of images the president has recently disseminated on Truth Social: Donald Trump on a horse next to George Washington on a horse, in the body of Hulk Hogan, beating Hogan in arm wrestling, standing between General George Patton and General Douglas MacArthur in a World War II uniform, hovering menacingly with a hockey stick over the Canadian prime minister, commanding the deck of an aircraft carrier, nuking parts of Earth from space, gleefully dancing to “YMCA” as he replaces a sign for Lake Ontario with one for Lake America. Maybe most surreal (though, admittedly, kind of funny), he posted a video of a squad of “Donald Ducks” with his signature hair, armed with machine guns, waddling in formation to protect said lake. Trump has never been shy about his proclivity for self-aggrandizement (daydreaming about having his face chiseled onto Mount Rushmore or printed on a $250 bill). But his recent spate of social-media posts—almost all products of AI—has him living in an expanded fantasyland. His supporters are likely to just giggle; more fun and trolling—so what? His critics might roll their eyes at the extravagant immaturity but almost can’t be bothered anymore (unless they’re Iceland). After a decade of Trump shenanigans, getting worked up either way is hard. Also, these images might come across as just a little sad—the projections of a man who is losing power and virility but desperately needs to see himself as powerful and virile. The absurd cartoons are worth pondering, though, and not for the sake of getting to know Trump better (I believe that onion has been thoroughly peeled). Because what AI can do to exaggerate and aggrandize Trump, it can do to everyone. For the president, this may be just one more level up on his escalator of self-regard, but for those without Napoleonic degrees of narcissism, the mental terrain opened up by these quick-prompt chimeras is new. When the psychoanalyst Jacques Lacan wanted to understand how the ego takes shape, he thought of mirrors. Sometime from six to 18 months of age, he theorized, a baby begins to recognize their reflection, and experiences what Lacan—abstruse French structuralist that he was—described as the “jubilant assumption of his specular image.” In plainer words, the baby realizes, That’s me! This is significant because the child at this point is otherwise a fragmented mess, experiencing the world through disconnected sensation. Lacan thought the mirror provides an idealized perception of wholeness when, really, inside it’s all a scramble, and through this illusion of coherence, a proper ego—a sense of self—begins to bud. Lacan didn’t believe that we ever really outgrow this reliance on our reflection, whether seen above a bathroom sink or in the ways other people look at us. But the mirror he had in mind could show you only the person who stands in front of it—the image is always tethered to physical reality. The AI mirror is something else. It asks what we’d like to see and produces it. In the past, only kings and emperors could indulge their most exalted, exaggerated picture of themselves. They could commission heroic, larger-than-life representations—though even these were limited by available materials and plausible realism. AI image-making has now democratized this kind of statue-building. I know because I just asked ChatGPT to create an image of me astride a bronze horse in the center of a city, and I’ve never looked more commanding. But AI can do so much more: The ideal self has escaped the confines of the mirror and even the gilded sculpture; it can now inflate to unimaginable dimensions. In this new world, our insecurities are not obstacles; they are starting points. Take my modest height. I can conjure up what I would look like in Victor Wembanyama’s stretched-taffy body—though I’d settle for something one foot shorter. I’m fully aware that this is just an image, and a bizarre one, but it engraves this taller me—a version who walks into a party and looks down, not up, at everyone—into my brain. It creates a gulf between reality and such a picture. This fantasy made manifest can either delude or taunt me. It exists, and I can’t forget it. Conversely, I should say, it also reveals what I’m most self-conscious about—exactly as AI does for Trump. The puffed up, dominating, victorious man with the six-pack is what the AI mirror shows the president, because that is what he prompts it to show him. (In most cases, of course, he is reposting images prompted by supporters, who are thereby exposing their own displaced fantasies.) To give Trump a little credit, he seems vaguely aware of the idea that AI is, like the Mirror of Erised in Harry Potter, showing us what we most want. One of his posts has Barack Obama looking into a mirror and asking: Mirror mirror on the wall who’s the greatest president of them all. No surprise, it’s Trump, who is staring back at him. Read: Let Trump keep building monuments to himself What I see in the AI mirror is greater than I could ever be—and infinitely editable. Instead of feeding a desire for coherence, as Lacan imagined, it creates a bottomless hunger for the unachievable. I can keep tweaking it with more prompts: “Give me thicker hair. Make my jaw more chiseled.” Start down this road, I’m afraid, and you’ll encounter unhappy young men using hammers to shape their actual faces so that they can come closer to the digital simulacra. Not all people are narcissists, and certainly not all are like Trump. But we are all susceptible. Who doesn’t dream occasionally of evading reality or the truth of the body you are stuck living in? On the scale of human history, it has been only about a second since we got photography (and the inevitable selfie) to augment what for so long only a pond’s surface or a piece of glass could show us: our own face. What will come from finding, instead, Supermen, kings, popes, and Jedi staring back at us? Without AI, Trump might be forced to look around him, perhaps at the portraits of other presidents in the White House—just like Richard Nixon did. In their book The Final Days, Bob Woodward and Carl Bernstein recounted what became a famous story: As Nixon’s presidency was crashing down around him, he began talking at night to these dead former presidents. (One of his sons-in-law denied this claim.) Oliver Stone dramatized the moment in his 1995 film, Nixon: The defeated, haggard president focuses on a painting of John F. Kennedy and says, with startling self-awareness, “When they look at you, they see what they want to be. When they look at me, they see what they are.” Fiction, I know. But this is the kind of reckoning that can happen when you have no choice but to see what is in front of you. Trump is also very aware of the presidents who came before him. In another recent post, he ranks them all in a tiered chart from Great (Lincoln, FDR, Jefferson, and so on) to Failures (Biden, Obama, Carter, and so on). And atop the entire graphic in a box roughly four times the size of all the others, Trump’s severe, unsmiling face looks out under a label that reads back to him: The Greatest.
The Atlantic
A Diagnosis for Social Conflict That Gets Lost Along the Way
2026-09-15

A Diagnosis for Social Conflict That Gets Lost Along the Way

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Weddings, famously, create conflict. Before I got married, my parents and I fought over the guest list. A friend of mine struggled with adding some of her husband’s Protestant traditions to their otherwise Jewish ceremony. Another friend had to decode, and then manage, complicated tensions among her future aunts- and uncles-in-law. All of these issues got resolved, and according to the conflict-resolution facilitator Priya Parker, the process of figuring them out made our weddings more successful expressions of our values. Parker explored the group creation of meaning in her 2018 best seller, The Art of Gathering. In her new book, The Art of Fighting, she writes that arguments are crucial to a group’s ability to do just that. What’s more, in American society—which Parker describes as both conflict averse and low on big rituals—high-stakes events such as weddings are among the “last remaining collective mechanisms to help us have the fights we need to have to figure out who we are now.” The Art of Fighting is predicated on Parker’s conviction that without confrontation, no group can work well. “Fighting,” she writes in her prologue, “is not the death of community. It is its lifeblood.” Parker considers Americans far too willing to focus on themselves rather than their communities, in large part due to a widespread fear of confrontation. A lot of loneliness, in her view, originates from the all-too-common decision to exit a challenging situation instead of dealing with its simmering disagreements. It’s a smart diagnosis, one that’s as relevant to small-bore social scenarios as it is to interactions within religious organizations, schools, and workplaces. The choice to hide from conflict is, to Parker, in many situations, a choice to be alone. Parker’s idea that conflict is inherently a social good is intriguing and counterintuitive. But The Art of Fighting is not nearly as communitarian a book as its setup would suggest. Although its more abstract passages describe healthy confrontation as helpful and worthy, its examples tell another story. Parker presents The Art of Fighting as a “group-help book” but tends to take a side in her case studies—and in most instances, her side wins. Read: A wedding reveals how much help is really available to you Her own life serves as illustration, too, and often provides the book’s most revealing moments. By way of arguing that everyone needs to get comfortable with disagreement, she mentions a clash she and her father had after the latter got upset that Parker hadn’t invited her stepbrother’s wife to her baby shower. As Parker relates it, their discussion of her reasoning—she and her stepsister-in-law just aren’t close—ends not with compromise or reconciliation but with the blunt lines: “He wasn’t getting it. And that was OK.” Such a one-sided resolution suggests a disinterest in compromise and an interpretation of fighting as an individualistic act. Facing conflict may very well be a social good—I believe it is—but her book more often seems to urge readers to wade into arguments to get what they want. The Art of Fighting is full of stories of conflict. Some appear only once, and some are threaded throughout the book. The quarreling groups that Parker features, many of which she worked with in her role as a facilitator, range in size from an extended family to a percussion ensemble to a large architecture firm. Parker uses every case to ask a variation of her central question: “How do you fight with those to whom you want to belong?” In the book’s most moving example, she writes about a church that, in the mid-1990s, deliberated about and then voted on whether it would perform and recognize gay marriages. Its pastor, Martin Copenhaver, understood that they couldn’t make a decision that might lead to division if his church were to survive. “In the political arena,” he tells Parker, “if you win by one vote, you win. In this arena, you win by one vote, you don’t win.” Hoping to forge meaningful consensus, he hosted four meetings to discuss the subject; only those who attended were allowed to vote. After the election—the church decided, not unanimously but with “no great division,” to allow gay marriage—he asked everyone to reconcile in person with someone who voted differently. In essence, Copenhaver organized the entire conflict to prioritize congregants’ investment in the church, not to lead them toward a certain outcome. I wanted to learn more about how other sorts of disputes, especially less orchestrated ones, might bolster a sense of community. But Parker is surprisingly blurry about the variety of incentives for confronting or ignoring tension that exist in one setting or another. Some strategies for managing conflict—waiting to engage until no one has steam pouring from their ears; ensuring that the arguing parties’ grievances and goals are clear; creating the right conditions for a tough conversation—may apply to all kinds of groups. But the book doesn’t do enough to acknowledge that different sorts of conflicts have different stakes. Starting a fight for the good of the group may be wise among relatives but foolish if doing so could also mean losing your job. And a values-based argument may not be worthwhile in a corporate context where the overall goal is to maximize profit. One of Parker’s recurring examples is a debate that took place within a law firm about whether the TV in its common space should show World Cup games played in Qatar. It begins with an intern objecting, on Slack, to the “human rights abuses of workers in the lead up to the World Cup and the horrific status of rights for LGBTQ people and women in Qatar.” The managing partners invited employees to what Parker characterizes as a too-wide conversation about the World Cup. It was, she writes, “the wrong fight with the wrong assemblage of people in the wrong room.” Parker uses this argument to underscore good and bad ways to navigate conflicts, especially ones that arise online—certainly an important issue in our screen-heavy times. But missing from this analysis is any real engagement on Parker’s part with the substance of the conflict itself. Readers never learn how the issue was resolved: Did the World Cup get shown or not? Was there ever a meaningful discussion about the objections the intern had raised? The book uses this example to describe strategies for handling internal dissent, focusing on how the managing partners could best have reestablished peace among their employees. It becomes a story about conflict management rather than conflict as a potential social good. Some of Parker’s personal anecdotes more vividly illustrate her not-quite-communitarian approach to fighting. In one, she describes enrolling her children and their cousin in a small soccer camp one summer. Initially, the kids report hating camp and getting bullied by their counselors. Parker speaks with the camp director, who talks with his employees and establishes that Parker’s children and niece, who started on the eighth week of camp, are unfamiliar with both the established cohort’s “teasing culture” and the basic rules of soccer. Parker suggests that the camp’s coaches “give our kids a Week One experience, orient them, do the things you did with all the other kids when they were new.” They comply, and the camp—which Parker needs for child care—works out. Parker’s reaction to the situation is entirely reasonable; what parent wouldn’t try to help a kid who’s being picked on, or scramble to rescue a child-care setup that seems about to fail? It is not, however, an illustration of confrontation as community service. She shows herself engaging in conflict to get her kids special treatment rather than nudging the kids to advocate for themselves or asking how the camp might better help them settle in despite their latecomer status. Another way to put this is that no compromise is on offer here, and we get no sense of a group working together. Parker, as the customer, gets to be right. Throughout The Art of Fighting, individuals prevail in this way. A woman named Rebekah, exhausted by attending her family’s Passover seders—too much cooking, too much disruption to routine, too much airtime for her right-wing uncle—gives them up. But Parker never writes about what Rebekah’s parents, siblings, or children might lose when she and her nuclear family stop coming, or how her continued, if conflicted, attendance might have led her relatives to create more sustainable traditions. Just as some therapists prioritize clients over the other people in their lives, Parker prioritizes the subjects of her anecdotes over those with whom they fight. What these stories add up to, ultimately, is a much less surprising take on conflict than the book tries to lay out. Parker provides a useful guide to having arguments in good faith without hurting anyone’s feelings unnecessarily or creating a corporate crisis. But arguments can be so much more than that. Alone in the book, the story of Copenhaver’s church demonstrates that even enduring and loaded disagreements can, if handled with enough care and respect, strengthen the bonds between parties—in other words, they can be productive, not just nondestructive. Many readers will have experienced this in intimate relationships. I, for one, have grown closer to some friends after air-clearing arguments and have drifted from others who weren’t willing or able to talk through issues when they arose. But as Parker points out, public life in the United States isn’t set up for healthy conflict. Meaningful group fights are rare experiences. Aside from my wedding planning, I haven’t been part of one. I believe, in theory, that such a dispute could be transformational for both a community and its individual members. But in order for Parker’s book to have taught me how to navigate one, she would have had to truly let go of the old idea that the reason to fight is to win. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission.
