What Comes After America
2026-09-15

What Comes After America

The Atlantic | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Emily St. John Mandel writes science fiction, with the accent firmly on fiction. Imagination, not technical verisimilitude, is her strength; she wisely disregards the scientific in favor of the human. Her indifference to plausibility is a measure of her confidence as a storyteller, and helps snare readers like me, who have a mild allergic reaction to postapocalyptic settings and outer-space odysseys. Her previous novel featured time travel, interplanetary travel, and a cosmic glitch; now, in her challenging new novel, Exit Party, we have accidental teleportation between parallel universes. The underlying theme, though, is irresistible: how shockingly little humanity understands about the sudden swerves and reversals that shape a life—or end it. The new book also offers a prescient invitation to consider the human toll that a world of chaos or repression can exact. Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. Exit Party’s stunning first sentence leaps ahead to 2031 and peeks into a woman’s soul: “In the first spring after the collapse of the United States, the blossoming of the jacaranda trees felt to Ari like grace.” Newly released after 18 years in prison, 48-year-old Ari Waker is just getting used to a Los Angeles that has seen vicious internecine violence. Although international peacekeeping troops have been withdrawn and the city’s curfew has been lifted, nothing like normalcy prevails. Ari walks with her roommate from their apartment in Echo Park (note the locale) to a party in Silver Lake. There, the incomprehensible happens: Two people from a parallel world materialize at the party, and another person (the host) is teleported in the opposite direction—all by accident. The inexplicable appearances and disappearances are as disorienting for the reader as they are for Ari, who wonders whether she has a brain tumor or is having a stroke. For the three teleported individuals, the experience is a disaster: instant, complete, and irreversible dislocation. In the alternate universe, we soon learn, the “United State” has become a fortress, “closed like a fist,” where the national doctrine is “Harmony at any cost.” There we encounter, among other characters, Ibari, an enforcer of the brutally repressive social order. Abrupt cataclysm is a signature of Mandel’s recent work. Before Exit Party came Sea of Tranquility (2022), The Glass Hotel (2020), and Station Eleven (2014)—all of them ingeniously splintered narratives involving many protagonists, zigzagging timelines, and some kind of shattering event. It’s never clear until the end that the shards of narrative can be assembled to form a coherent story. The big picture that at last emerges in Exit Party features paired life stories of doppelgängers, genetically identical sets of characters who have made decisions in their divergent worlds that move them along radically different paths. Ari and Ibari are the principal “twins.” Though at first they seem utterly dissimilar, they are, as Ibari recognizes late in the novel, “two sides of the same coin.” From the March 2020 issue: Ruth Franklin on Emily St. John Mandel’s ‘Station Eleven’ and ‘The Glass Hotel’ Repeatedly drawn to criminality (which she associates with freedom), Ari has had more than her share of “hinge moments,” the term she prefers for dramatic, world- and life-changing twists. She launched one criminal enterprise—selling luxury goods bought with stolen credit-card numbers—as a teenager, while living in Brooklyn with an aunt and uncle who dealt in stolen antiques. Soon enough, helped out by her cousin, she was selling U.S. passports. When the Russian Mafia lured her into smuggling people, her cousin, beset by moral qualms, balked. So she hired an assassin to kill him. That’s how she landed in prison. Sprung from jail and hoping to find her footing, Ari drifts instead. She works as a dishwasher and in warehouses, and then becomes, in dizzying succession, a gunrunner, a trophy wife, a philanthropist, an art collector. Arriving in Paris in a car with bulletproof windows (another hinge moment), she wonders, “How many completely separate lives am I going to get to live before I run out of chances.” Mandel makes this resilient character—a serial transgressor with a pragmatic code of ethics, uncannily accurate premonitions, and a touching capacity for regret—sympathetic and compelling. Her last name, Waker, is a clue to her essence: She’s awake, aware. She will always notice the jacarandas. A meticulous observer by profession, Ibari is 79 when we meet her, and has risen to a high rank in the Internal Security Service. She’s as frank about her complicity in the disappearance of rule-breaking fellow citizens as Ari is about her weakness for criminality. Ibari has nonetheless imagined a scenario in which, instead of running surveillance operations for the State, she became a criminal; like her twin, she felt there would be “freedom in that.” The door that swings open at the party remains thereafter obdurately shut. Appalled, puzzled, fascinated, Ari and Ibari (in their parallel worlds) try their best to comprehend what has happened. They investigate, quizzing the accidentally teleported. Eventually, they (and the befuddled reader) learn enough about each other and the different worlds they inhabit to have some thoughts about “entanglement”—about how doubles in their respective universes intersect. Which they do, eerily. Even before she knows anything about Ibari and her career in the secret police, Ari wonders why she herself hadn’t “just become a detective, instead of turning to crime.” She can see the appeal of a life played out in the ranks of the NYPD. She yearns for a homeland where she would be spared the “permanent unease” of encroaching anarchy, where armed fighters aren’t forever shooting at one another on the edge of town. In Exit Party, those who diverge nearly converge, and every echo resonates. When they were young girls, Ari and Ibari each read the same children’s detective novel and both latched on to the same sentence: “True diligence means following the improbable path.” While in prison, Ari had TRUE DILIGENCE tattooed on her left wrist; to judge from the improbable path that Ari and Ibari’s creator has followed, it’s Mandel’s motto too. After growing up in western Canada, mostly homeschooled, Mandel graduated in 2000 from the School of Toronto Dance Theatre. Nine years later, at 30, having abandoned her dream of dancing professionally, she published her debut novel. Like her next two books, it was a noir thriller; all three slipped by more or less unnoticed. Her fourth, Station Eleven, about a flu pandemic “that exploded like a neutron bomb over the surface of the earth,” killing almost every human, won prizes and much-deserved critical praise, and sold remarkably well—before getting a big bump six years later during COVID. Sales of the book passed the 1 million mark shortly after the first episode of an HBO adaptation aired, in late 2021. Station Eleven was Mandel’s own hinge moment. In addition to introducing what has since become her fiction’s hallmark fracturing of time and lives, the novel displayed her calm authority and her easy way with ambitious imaginative gambits—one world erased, another fighting to be born—carried off with barely a trace of writerly flourish: “The caravans had once been pickup trucks, but now they were pulled by teams of horses on wheels of steel and wood.” In Mandel’s work, unadorned realism is at the service of baroque invention, and everything, including the prose, is subordinate to the story. Like all the best stories, hers are character-driven. In Station Eleven, Kirsten Raymonde, a child actor before the pandemic and afterward a skilled warrior, is canny and resourceful in the face of catastrophe. Eight years old when her familiar world ended, and a semi-feral teenager when she hooked up with a ragtag troupe that stages Shakespeare plays and orchestral concerts, Kirsten is permanently traumatized, ferocious when necessary, and somehow still capable of tenderness. She’s a sturdy hook for the reader. Mandel holds tight to some of her characters: She teleports a few between the parallel worlds of her novels. Ari made her debut in The Singer’s Gun (2010), where she was already mercilessly pragmatic and minimally sentimental. An important prop in the new novel is a painting by a minor but vivid character in The Glass Hotel, Lucas Alkaitis, a heroin-addicted artist, talented and doomed, “on the run from mediocrity and Brylcreem and grey flannel suits.” Lucas himself also reappears, still an addict, still committed to his art, in a curious coda to Exit Party set in his Manhattan studio in 1960—a final, wrenching narrative swerve. In Sea of Tranquility, you’ll find a Mandel stand-in: a newly famous writer in the year 2203 who has left her moon-colony home for planet Earth to embark on a globe-spanning book tour. Separated from her family, she finds herself unmoored, homesick. An interviewer in Estonia asks her what it’s like to have published a tremendously successful book. She answers, “I wrote three books that no one noticed, no distribution beyond the moon colonies, and then … it’s like slipping into a parallel universe.” She continues, “I somehow fell into a bizarre upside-down world where people actually read my work. It’s extraordinary. I hope I never get used to it.” One of the projects of modernism (and postmodernism) is to unsettle our confidence in the coherence of the self and thereby reveal the truth about our disjointed identity, so often sabotaged by an unruly unconscious. The so-called neomodernist novelist Rachel Cusk has declared that she is “not interested in character because I don’t think character exists anymore.” It seems to me that Mandel, who merrily jettisons narrative conventions and plays cunningly with timelines, nonetheless insists on the essential coherence of her fictional creations. How many completely separate lives can Ari live and still track as a character? “I took out a contract on my cousin,” she says to the host of the Silver Lake party. “It was a mistake and I’m not that person anymore.” I don’t believe her. Though she probably would not now pay to have a close relative murdered, she remains the ruthless person who once did. Read: Emily St. John Mandel’s writing advice All of the doubling that powers the plot of Exit Party prompts us to consider how we recognize ourselves and others—whether we believe in the integrity of our persona and theirs. What is the essential stuff of our identity? What makes us whole? In The Singer’s Gun, Mandel has Anton Waker, the cousin Ari thought she wanted dead, provide an answer: It’s details, “small and personal and utterly beautiful and mundane, that make up the fabric of a person’s life.” Hinge moments may matter less than humdrum daily doings. We change tack, break free, and start over, yet we carry with us the stubborn imprint of all our old habits, the jumbled storehouse of memory, and a long trail of sensory data so familiar that it goes unnoticed. We endure. And we suffer, due to circumstances beyond our control. This is where the doubling of the radically different nations comes in. Which is less awful, the calm and sinister surveillance state that Ibari serves with growing reluctance, or the “fracturing continent” that Ari yearns to escape? At first Ari reacts to chaos with a rueful calm—“You don’t notice the supply chain till it collapses”—but after years of “flashes of gunfire at night” from militia skirmishes that “stopped just short of all-out war,” a bone-crushing weariness sets in. As she knows, “Societal breakdown is exhausting.” The “malevolent weight” of a tyrannical government is similarly sapping. When the secret police is everywhere, Ibari wryly notes, “anyone can be surveilling you at any time, including the person whom you are yourself surveilling.” To live peacefully, you make believe everything’s fine, ignoring “black cars moving too slowly down sunlit streets, unsmiling plainclothes agents in pairs, drones gliding quietly through the air.” At the academy where she trained to join the secret police, Ibari was told, “A harmonious society comes at a price.” The instructor added, “And we are not just the ones who exact that price, we are the ones who pay it.” Mandel declines to identify any specific ideology in either world. No dictator is referred to in the totalitarian regime; no feuding warlords are named in the failed state. In both places, the status quo has evolved without recognizable human agency. That’s simply how it is—for now. Ibari comes to treasure the phrase “No country lasts forever,” because “there was such hope in that.” A bitter sort of hope, but one that chimes with the spirit of the novel as it winds down, attuned to fluid time as well as fluid space. With Edna St. Vincent Millay’s “famous little poem” about burning the candle at both ends in mind, the artist Lucas Alkaitis (soon to die of a heroin overdose) reflects, “The point isn’t that the candle won’t last the night. Everything is temporary. The point is that the light is gorgeous while it lasts.” This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “What Comes After America.” ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. I don't know about any particular phone, but in many devices the long press power off is an old-fashioned kill switch that charges a capacitor, eventually reaching the voltage to turn off a transistor connected to a "power good" line on the power management IC. And inside the IC it's the same deal. So as long as the button works, it should be able to hard power off, even if the software is badly bugged. Actually numerous times I've seen people complaining their phone or tablet wouldn't turn off, when actually the software was just frozen and they didn't know about long press power off. "May I see that?" "Wow... that's amazing!" "To you, maybe..." Not necessarily a capacitor; sometimes a little counter circuit, on the MCU but independent of the CPU and not controllable (or vetoable) by it. In either case, not prone to systemic failures of the higher-level logic. People overestimate the difficulty of adding low-level contingencies to high-level systems, and underestimate engineers' eagerness to do so. But this is the sort of thing that EEs worry about all the time. When I'm worried my phone doesn't turn itself off, I just use a small flyswatter. (But it's expensive! I really ought to try this long press thing one day.) We bought some Dell towers last year where even the long press of the power button can apparently be overridden by software. I was updating several dozen of them using fwupdmgr (followed by a reboot), but one of them never completed the update after rebooting. The Luna experiment illustrates an important distinction between task automation and organizational autonomy. An agent may be capable of interacting with email, browsers, telephones, financial systems, and other operational tools, yet still lack the higher-level judgment required to operate a viable business. Retail, for example, requires continuous decisions about demand, inventory, pricing, customer behavior, risk, and capital allocation. These are not simply interface problems. The vending-machine incident demonstrates a related issue: increasing an agent's operational authority also increases the potential consequences of poorly specified objectives, adversarial inputs, and unforeseen edge cases. Giving an autonomous system access to financial resources therefore changes the problem from "Can the agent perform the task?" to "Can the agent reliably determine which actions should be performed?" That makes Pion an interesting research direction. A platform that enables many independent experiments could provide considerably more evidence about the practical limits of autonomous agents than a small number of carefully controlled demonstrations. The ultimate measure of success, however, should not be whether an agent can technically operate a company. It should be whether the resulting organization can make sound decisions, remain economically viable, respond appropriately to unexpected conditions, and produce value for actual customers. In other words, autonomy is not merely the ability to act without a human in the loop. It is the ability to exercise useful judgment when the correct action is not already specified. In other words, autonomy is not merely the ability to act without a human in the loop. Summer loads typically peak mid-day and follow solar generation closely... batteries help make managing supply and demand easier, but huge capacity isn't necessarily needed or helpful. By contrast, it's winter loads that experience the morning/evening "double peak". That's where these large capacities come into play... the sun directly warms houses during the middle of the day (cutting the need for heating) and the batteries soak up excess dollar generation, which then gets spent as the sun sets and in the morning hours the next day. 1. You're right the article is light on details. They did install 17,000 MW worth of batteries; ~13,500 was grid-scale storage and the rest was residential supported through rebates. The rebates on the batteries you earn over time, and to do so you must export some of your stored battery power back to the grid during peak hours. So about 15-20% was residential people incentivized to use their home batteries as storage and the rest was grid storage. The article doesn't point out that California also added 27,000 MW of generation capacity also but good news it was almost entirely renewables, wind and solar mostly; natural gas power generation has been flat in it's power generation capacity. They still need the NG to help deal with the hourly volatility of the grid, but they didn't need to add any and overall usage is down around 50%. And to your point, the other thing they did in the interim periods is halfway through this (2022) they asked people to voluntarily use less power via these Flex Alerts, sending the population alerts to limit usage. 2023 it was a colder year on average so they didn't have to go that path. The Supreme Court’s Conservatives and Liberals Just Came to the Brink—and Finally Said No
2026-09-15

The Supreme Court’s Conservatives and Liberals Just Came to the Brink—and Finally Said No

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. There are two ways of looking at the Supreme Court’s three-sentence ruling in USPS v. California Monday evening, which put an end to Donald Trump’s attempt to disenfranchise millions of elderly, disabled, military, rural, Native American, and other voters in the 2026 midterm elections who depend upon vote by mail to cast a ballot. The first is to lament that the government pushed this case to its limits—going not once, not twice, but three times to the high court—and that two justices, Samuel Alito and Clarence Thomas, were willing to simply ignore mass disenfranchisement and side with Trump’s bogus voter-fraud crusade. The second is to note that the court’s conservatives and liberals came to the brink, saw that ruling the other way risked serious election subversion, and said no. For the time being, let’s celebrate that the center of the court did hold, even as dangers remain ahead in both 2026 and 2028. Slate readers are already familiar with this long saga, so just a brief review is necessary. Back in March, Trump put out an executive order on voting (his second since returning to office in 2025) that, among other things, directed the Postal Service to establish a rule imposing a new set of requirements for states that aimed to continue having their ballots delivered by mail. The rule, issued in late August, mandated that states receive USPS approval for new envelope designs with intelligent bar codes on them, print those bar codes on each envelope, upload a list of their voters to a USPS portal, and submit the ballots; the agency would then check the names encoded in the bar codes against those in the states’ voter lists. The rule further stipulated that USPS should reject entire batches of ballots if there was one potential mismatch. During the brief time when courts had not yet blocked the rule from taking effect, USPS rejected the ballot design of Leon County, Florida, because its required printed notation about return mail was 0.014 of an inch too close to other information on the envelope. Now, complying with all these new regulations would be pretty challenging for election administrators to do, even with lead time and practice. But the government tried to impose the rules for ongoing voting. Envelopes have already been loaded with ballots and addressed. Indeed, North Carolina began mailing ballots on Sept. 4, and other states are doing so now. Even as of Monday’s SCOTUS ruling, USPS did not have a working portal for states to upload their voter lists, not even on a voluntary basis, something the agency had said would be possible by last week. The upshot of USPS’s administrative failure is that the rule would have stopped the agency from accepting ballots from all states because there could be no matching between the envelope and the portal. That means that, across the country, the most vulnerable voters who depend on vote by mail would have been disenfranchised, including core Republican constituencies. The federal district court issued a preliminary injunction partly on this basis, noting the burdens on election administrators and the public interest costs. The court also found that the rule exceeded USPS’s authority to issue. The Supreme Court’s terse order agreed on both points. It held that the government was likely to lose on the legal question of USPS’s authority. And it held that the “equitable factors”—that is, the burden on election administrators and the voting public—outweighed the government’s purported interest in stopping voter fraud. White-hot fury that the government tried so hard to get this rule in place for the 2026 elections, knowing full well the consequences had it been successful at the Supreme Court, is the correct emotion. A good legal brief takes on the strongest argument of the other side and deals with it head-on. Here, the government had to overcome the district court’s factual findings about the unfairness of the rule. But the government’s brief simply ignored the mass disenfranchisement point, one flagged by the states and by plaintiffs from voting rights organizations, as well as in an amicus brief written by professors, a document that the Safeguarding Democracy Project filed in this case. The failure to address the mass disenfranchisement point essentially conceded it, and that was enough for the court majority. But it was not enough for Alito, dissenting along with Thomas. Alito first concluded that the U.S. was likely to win on the merits. When he turned to the equities, he parroted the empty and unsupported claim that the USPS guidelines would stop fraud. On the other side of the ledger, he asserted that states were divided on the equities because some Trump-supporting state attorneys general had filed a brief siding with the government; never mind that some of those states’ chief election officers filed on the other side. In any event, he went on to blame the states for litigating the case so hard that it somehow stopped the government from getting its rule and portal up and running; he also, once again, implicitly blamed Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson for slow-walking the case when it first came before the justices in July. Totally absent from Alito’s balancing were the voters. They weren’t an afterthought; their interests were not considered at all. This is unsurprising, considering that the justice has done more to hurt the cause of voting and voting rights than anyone else on the court in at least a century. So there are good reasons to be furious and worried about what this portends. But let’s not be all doom and gloom. We know that Justice Brett Kavanaugh, who agreed, in a separate concurrence, that the government might win on the merits, saw that the equities favored the voters this time around. And we can be sure that at least two of the court’s other conservatives agreed too, along with the court’s three liberals, on both the merits and the equities. A third conservative justice either agreed with the majority or did not see fit to side with Alito’s dissent or Kavanaugh’s concurrence. That ain’t nothing. Just as in the period after the 2020 presidential election, when Trump tried to subvert the outcome with bogus legal arguments and conspiracy theories, the Supreme Court again stood firm and said no. Back then, it rejected without comment an original petition from Texas AG Ken Paxton to throw out the results from some states that had voted for President Joe Biden. Now it has failed to countenance a mass disenfranchisement just weeks before a critical midterm. The Supreme Court has been terrible on voting rights, but so far it has held the line on actual election rigging. Let’s celebrate that, even as we gird for more battles to come. Trump Wants to Prevent Future Presidents From Fighting Climate Change
2026-09-14

Trump Wants to Prevent Future Presidents From Fighting Climate Change

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. It’s a great day for people who hate the idea of a livable planet. For everyone else, not so much. The Trump administration today plans to both rescind existing rules on climate pollution from power plants and undercut future administrations’ ability to regulate those emissions. These moves face court challenges. But if they go through, they would gut what’s left of U.S. climate policy and undermine efforts to stave off the worst effects of climate change. What do the new Trump rules actually say? Both the Obama and Biden administrations used the Clean Air Act to try to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, which drive climate change. Now Donald Trump, who has labeled climate change a “con job” and twice pulled out of international agreements meant to mitigate it, is reversing those efforts. One of his new rules would reportedly eliminate Biden-era regulations that required power plants that run on coal and natural gas to limit their carbon emissions. If that rule matches a version reporters obtained last year, it will argue that power plants don’t contribute significantly to pollution or climate change because they’re “a small and decreasing part of global emissions,” even though coal remains the worst-polluting fossil fuel. The second proposal would be even more far-reaching. It’s expected to claim that power plant emissions in general don’t meet the Clean Air Act’s standard for Environmental Protection Agency regulation. The power industry is, behind transportation, the second-largest source of planet-warming emissions in the United States and the second-largest producer of greenhouse gas emissions in the world. Kneecapping the EPA’s ability to regulate the industry under the Clean Air Act would gut one of the main ways Democratic presidents have tried to minimize climate impacts and tie future Democratic presidents’ hands. What else is Trump doing to fuel climate change? Earlier this year, Trump rescinded a long-standing EPA finding that greenhouse gases jeopardize human health. That determination, known as the “endangerment finding,” had been the basis of federal climate regulation for nearly two decades. Without it, the administration has effectively given itself carte blanche to go after all manner of climate regulations. Trump has lifted restrictions on vehicle tailpipe emissions, canceled billions for clean energy projects in blue states, and delayed efforts to eliminate chemicals that are hundreds or thousands of times better at trapping heat than carbon dioxide. How is the administration justifying this? Trump officials argue that climate rules burden the fossil fuel industry, hurt the economy, and increase what Americans pay for electricity. In truth, the EPA’s own assessments recently found that repealing power-plant pollution standards will do little to lower utility bills or save the coal industry, which has been in decline for decades. But it would be a major favor to the GOP’s fossil fuel benefactors, saving the industry billions. The economic impacts for the rest of us will probably be imperceptible, particularly when compared to the damage Trump’s moves threaten to do to the environment and to public health. Surely there’s something that people worried about climate change can do about these new rules? Democratic-led states and environmental organizations will sue over these rules, as they have over the others. Those challenges could take years to resolve. But the Supreme Court seems likely to hear at least some of them, and its conservative majority has shown a blasé attitude toward climate change. In 2016, Chief Justice John Roberts led a behind-the-scenes effort to block the Clean Power Plan, an Obama initiative to slash emissions, in part citing “irreparable harm” to energy companies. In 2022, the court narrowed the EPA’s ability to regulate power plant emissions, forcing Joe Biden to adopt more-piecemeal measures. That doesn’t sound promising. So what would it mean if these rules took effect? Think of the fight against climate change like a tug-of-war, with emissions on one end of the rope and efforts to mitigate them on the other. Trump’s moves would tilt the playing field toward emissions. This means not only larger and faster changes to the climate but also an increase in other health effects due to the pollution that fossil-fuel-burning power plants spew, which can negatively affect anyone with lungs. The Biden administration estimated that its power plant rules would’ve prevented up to 1,200 premature deaths and 360,000 asthma attacks in 2035 alone. Some Democratic-led states have stepped into the breach, pursuing tougher rules of their own and even trying to force fossil fuel companies to pay for damage from wildfires, floods, and other climate-related disasters. Still, states probably can’t balance out a president who’s doing everything in his power to spur climate change along. And time is of the essence. Pondering the potential heat death of humanity is a bummer of a way to start the week, I know. To get your mind off it for the rest of the night, my colleagues suggest: TV worth rewatching: What is it about the HBO miniseries Band of Brothers that keeps viewers coming back for more, 25 years after it originally aired? Slate’s Rebecca Onion investigates why fans are so attached to the show. This game show tradition was Alex Trebek’s favorite: What is Jeopardy!’s annual Teen Tournament? Claire McNear, America’s preeminent Jeopardy! reporter, did a deep dive into the beloved event—which disappeared after Trebek’s death—and got some news about its future. Some snack science: Consumer Reports recently published a scary-sounding investigation on chemicals present in common kids’ snacks. Epidemiologist Gideon Meyerowitz-Katz breaks down the science and concludes that the results are actually pretty reassuring. A three-ingredient treat: If you’d rather nosh on a less processed snack, Noah Lederman recommends a super-fresh dish that’s perfect for late summer: a heavenly concoction he’s dubbed Fish Crudo With Melon and Tomato Water. OK, that’s all from me. Happy munching, and see you tomorrow! Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. The Trump Proposal to Pay Moms for Childcare May Sound Like a Good Thing. But There’s a Truly Disturbing Catch.
2026-09-14

The Trump Proposal to Pay Moms for Childcare May Sound Like a Good Thing. But There’s a Truly Disturbing Catch.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-14

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. For decades now, American feminists have asked our government to do more to support families and children. We have agitated for paid parental leave, universal childcare, better public schools, child allowances, and other help for struggling parents. Many feminists, especially socialist ones, have demanded wages for housework as well as childcare—salaries, in other words, for mothers who stay home. Now the Trump administration is reportedly doing something along those lines: extending financial support to mothers who choose to care for their children at home rather than put them in daycare. Good news, right? Wrong. The exact details of the plan are not yet official, but the gist has been shared with reporters. And as it turns out, the administration isn’t expanding support to parents at all. The policy will draw from funds that currently largely cover the care of children of low-income single moms. What’s more, the new policy will benefit only mothers who are married. It’s something like an anti-feminist reverse–Robin Hood: Take from poor single mothers to give to more stable ones. In the 1990s, the government created a program to help low-income families afford childcare so that parents could work or go to school. The benefit is fairly modest, averaging about $9,000 per child annually, in a country where childcare costs can exceed $30,000 a year. The overwhelming majority—80 percent—of the recipients of this federal childcare credit are single parents, and the overwhelming majority of them are single mothers. Given the Trump administration’s emphasis on this plan as a boon for stay-at-home mothers, you may wonder if the single moms presently in the workforce could use childcare funds to pay themselves to stay home. The answer is no. If the new policy is implemented, the payment would apply solely to married stay-at-home moms. The single mothers who rely now on the childcare subsidies would have to keep working (i.e., meet the current requirement to get the subsidy) or marry a gainfully employed man. (If only they had thought of that!) Parents who are unmarried by cohabitating also wouldn’t qualify for the stay-at-home benefit. Married mothers, though, could collect a government payment to stay home, so long as their husbands work full time and for pay. (The rule applies to parents, but the vast majority of stay-at-home parents in America are stay-at-home moms, and conservatives are explicit to state that they believe it’s best if mothers stay home and men work for pay.) Conservatives might argue that the government has no business paying single mothers to stay home. And fair enough—but then it shouldn’t be paying married ones to stay home either. And, worse, the administration is contemplating expanding funding eligibility without actually increasing the amount of money up for grabs. That would mean that there would be “more parents competing for the same dollars,” as one fellow at a conservative think tank put it. As a result, some money that is currently earmarked largely for poor single moms will end up going to mothers with working husbands who choose to stay home. This initiative is transparently not about helping all children to have a parent at home if that is what their parents desire. If it were, then it would apply to all lower-income families equally, regardless of whether a breadwinner spouse is in the picture. This is the government paying only for married moms to stay home. Single moms apparently don’t deserve that luxury. This is especially galling given that conservatives correctly point out that children of married couples tend to do better than children of single parents. Children raised in two-parent families in which one parent is employed full time already have a host of advantages, while children raised in low-income single-parent households are more likely to face significant challenges. Why in the world would we take from the least privileged to give to the group conservatives themselves say are the best off? The only explanation is that this isn’t really about supporting children. It’s about rewarding some mothers and punishing others. This is all part of a broader conservative effort to push women out of the workforce and back into the home, as well as into traditional family structures. Earlier this week, Katie Miller (wife of White House deputy chief of staff for policy Stephen Miller) lamented how “terrible” it is that women now outnumber men in the workforce—a comment she made on The Katie Miller Podcast, which is her job. In the Heritage Foundation proposal, titled “Saving America by Saving the Family,” that seems to be the blueprint for this proposed Trump policy, the group argues: “In promoting new family policies, one must be mindful of mistakes of the past. For example, credits designed specifically to benefit poor single mothers may be well intended, but they have proven to incentivize single motherhood in poor communities and trap women there through marriage penalties.” The result, according to the proposal, is that “the growth of the federal government displaced men from their traditional role as providers. Welfare moved the primary responsibility to provide for a family from fathers to the government. … The expansion of welfare programs, particularly AFDC, made the federal government the de facto husband for millions of poor women across the country.” The implication is that the federal government should stop supporting poor single moms so that they might hitch themselves to a man, and that the federal government should financially reward women who follow the traditional script. The Heritage Foundation is particularly explicit in this aim. Its version of a policy that would support stay-at-home parents would actually impose income minimums for a household to qualify—in other words, the support would go not to the poorest families but to those who are more stable. Heritage proposes tax breaks that would apply only to two-parent married families with one stay-at-home spouse, and that would offer even more generous payments when those families have multiple children. It also proposes special bank accounts exclusively for people who marry young (before the age of 30). And Heritage is explicit that this shift in resources (which also involves ending the Head Start program) is part of a broader right-wing project that includes attacks on contraception, abortion, in vitro fertilization, and LGBTQ+ rights. From “Saving America by Saving the Family”: “A large share of the blame for the present malady lies in moral and cultural trends that have both fed and been fed by government policy. Chief among these is surely the sexual revolution, which separated the sex act from marriage and childbearing. It is not just that sexual mores were revolutionized. Technology and policy also played key supporting roles. Starting in the 1960s, the Pill and other contraceptives swept the country—which promised to reduce the unwanted consequences of casual sex with multiple partners across a lifetime.” The Heritage report points a finger at no-fault divorce, child protection laws, and government secularism as further causes of our culture’s alleged decline. And it really zeros in on women going to college: “Social pressures, from popular culture to guidance counselors to parents, now encourage almost everyone to go to college and delay getting married and having children,” the report states, before spotlighting a study that found that “women who complete 4 years of college are less likely to have a child, while completion rates of 4 years of college rose 10 percent for women over the past decade.” The connection is clear: Conservatives want more women to marry and have babies; women going to college is an impediment to that. If we’re giving parents money for childcare, it does indeed seem fair to allow that money to support at-home care too, whether the carer is the child’s parent or a nanny. The French system does something like this. It offers paid parental leave, then a variety of care options afterward, largely cost-adjusted according to family income. Parents who wish to stay home may qualify for some cash; parents who want to hire a nanny may qualify for a subsidized rate; parents who want to send their children to France’s much-beloved crèches can do that too, paying an amount that varies according to what they can afford. If “pro-family” conservatives were pushing this kind of system, I promise you feminists would cheer—even those, like me, who think it’s generally a good thing when women work. But that isn’t what this Trump policy does. This policy is essentially a financial reward system for married stay-at-home mothers, funded at the expense of poor single moms. It’s punishing the children of women whose lives don’t align with the right-wing feminine ideal. And it is part of a larger agenda to push women out of school, out of the workforce, into early motherhood, and into the home—whether we want to be there or not. New device detects traces of nuclear material in air ducts, enhances safety at fuel cycle facilities
2026-09-15

New device detects traces of nuclear material in air ducts, enhances safety at fuel cycle facilities

Tech Xplore - electronic gadgets, technology advances and research news | 2026-09-15

America is building datacenters faster than the grid can power them
2026-09-15

America is building datacenters faster than the grid can power them

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Meeting expected energy consumption through 2030 will require $110 billion in new generation resources Financial service biz Moody's says the availability of power is still a major constraint on US datacenter growth as the pace of construction exceeds the speed at which the nation's grid infrastructure can add the requisite capacity. America is the epicenter of the datacenter construction boom, driven by the AI craze and demand for compute capacity to train ever-larger foundation models. The latest report from Moody's estimates the power needed by all those bit barns will reach 426 terawatt-hours (TWh) in 2030, citing data from the International Energy Agency (IEA). That is almost double the 2025 figure. It falls on the power sector to develop new resources to meet this extra demand as traditional power generation is struggling to keep pace. Moody's calculates that building the additional generation capacity will cost about $110 billion, adding an estimated $25 billion to $30 billion a year to electricity system costs. Yet efforts to supply the rapidly growing number of cloud and AI server farms are running up against lengthy permitting processes and long lead times for key equipment and materials, causing delays of up to seven years in some cases. Management consultancy Bain & Company highlighted the problem nearly two years ago when it warned that energy companies needed to significantly boost their generation capacity, reversing years of flat or declining demand. Transmission buildout is a parallel bottleneck, with $80 billion to $115 billion in grid investment through 2030 already approved or under construction across US power markets, Moody's says, with the potential to triple over the next decade. Investment bank Jefferies reported in June that half of the extra US datacenter capacity planned for 2026 is unlikely to come online this year, blaming power availability and grid connection setbacks, along with other issues including zoning and permitting challenges. Regions able to accelerate development, such as Texas, have experienced a wave of datacenter construction. The resulting pressure on local grids has prompted some authorities to clamp down on new connections for compute sheds. Moody's believes most new IT campus operators are still likely to seek grid connections to limit development costs and benefit from service reliability and shared redundancy. Yet many are also seeking alternatives, including on-site behind-the-meter (BTM) power generation to avoid delays in bringing facilities online. The document also notes that the growing energy demands from server farms are inflating power prices and customer bills in some regions of America, as The Register reported earlier this year. This has led to affordability concerns, fueling opposition to datacenters and prompting measures such as stricter approval frameworks and moratoriums on new projects. President Trump even introduced a Ratepayer Protection Pledge, under which tech giants and energy companies committed to shielding consumers from rising energy costs due to the datacenter boom. In the long term, Moody's believes that large-scale infrastructure investment will ultimately strengthen grid reliability and affordability, despite the risks. Extra generation capacity will alleviate longstanding supply constraints, it says, reducing price volatility during periods of extreme weather or high demand, while expanded transmission networks could improve access to lower-cost generation resources, particularly when datacenter developers or their tenants help finance the associated infrastructure. ® Meeting expected energy consumption through 2030 will require $110 billion in new generation resources Attackers already exploiting the critical flaw, and Cisco warns they may be able to cover their tracks once they're in PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Nitter's repository archived as of September 11, 2026 Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Cisco email security boxes can be rooted by... an email
2026-09-15

Cisco email security boxes can be rooted by... an email

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Attackers already exploiting the critical flaw, and Cisco warns they may be able to cover their tracks once they're in Criminals are exploiting a critical Cisco Secure Email Gateway flaw that can turn a malicious email into root access. The vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-76461, carries a 9.8 CVSS score and affects physical and virtual Secure Email Gateway appliances regardless of their configuration. Cisco says there are no workarounds, so patching is the only fix. The bug lies in how Cisco's AsyncOS software handles incoming email. An attacker doesn't need to log in: they can send a booby-trapped message through a vulnerable gateway and, if the exploit works, run commands as root. Which is not exactly what you want from the box tasked with keeping nasty emails out. Cisco's Product Security Incident Response Team said it became aware of active exploitation in September, although the networking giant hasn't said who is behind the attacks, how long they have been going on, or how many organizations have been compromised. Cisco uncovered the bug while resolving a Technical Assistance Center support case. Signs suggest at least some Cisco cloud customers were caught up in the attacks. Cisco said it investigated devices belonging to its Secure Email Cloud service and directly contacted customers whose appliances showed indicators of possible compromise. It is now carrying out remediation and recovery work and says all of its Secure Email Cloud devices have been upgraded to AsyncOS 16.5.0-780. Admins running their own appliances have a little more work to do. Cisco recommends checking logs for signs of suspicious activity, but warns that finding nothing doesn't necessarily mean the system is clean. Once attackers have root access, Cisco says they could tamper with the logs and cover their tracks. Admins are also being told to check network and firewall logs for anything unusual, rather than relying on the gateway itself for answers. For virtual appliances suspected of being compromised, Cisco's recovery advice is fairly drastic: preserve the forensic evidence, deploy a fresh VM running fixed software, rebuild the configuration, and rotate credentials and cryptographic material. Cisco has fixed the bug in AsyncOS releases 15.5.5-014, 16.0.4-302 and 16.5.0-780, with customers strongly encouraged to move to the latter. There's still a decent-sized target pool out there. The Shadowserver Foundation was tracking more than 400 Cisco Secure Email Gateway appliances exposed to the internet as of Monday The flaw has also landed in CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog, with US federal civilian agencies ordered to remediate it by September 17. CVE-2026-76461 comes less than a year after attackers exploited another critical AsyncOS flaw, CVE-2025-20393, to break into Cisco Secure Email Gateway appliances and install persistence mechanisms. That bug eventually scored a perfect 10. For anyone still running an affected gateway, the message is fairly simple: the box designed to inspect hostile email can itself be pwned by one; attackers are already doing it, and there is no workaround to hide behind.  ® Meeting expected energy consumption through 2030 will require $110 billion in new generation resources Attackers already exploiting the critical flaw, and Cisco warns they may be able to cover their tracks once they're in PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Nitter's repository archived as of September 11, 2026 Downloading a model is increasingly easy. Twitter workaround worked around again: X's lawyers cancel XCancel again
2026-09-15

Twitter workaround worked around again: X's lawyers cancel XCancel again

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Nitter's repository archived as of September 11, 2026 Nitter and XCancel, a pair of social media services which let you view posts on X without an X account, are down again after the latest round of legal wrangling. While the XCancel site now displays the message "Unfortunately, due to a new development in the ongoing legal proceedings, we are required to suspend this service again until further notice," Nitter's site is offline, and the code repository was archived on September 11, 2026. Things were not looking good in August for the services, which allowed a user to view posts on X (formerly Twitter) without an account or having to endure adverts and accept trackers. X's lawyers fired off a cease-and-desist letter, and the services acquiesced, going offline while the implications were digested. A few weeks later, the services were back up and running. At the time, zedeus, Nitter's maintainer, wrote, "an announcement will be made within a week, once we're able to reveal more information." Alas, that "more information" appears to be another cessation. XCancel wrote simply, "We can't share more details," and added a link to X for users keen for content. It is difficult to say for sure if and when the services might be back, or if users have headed elsewhere for their microblogging fix. Users not wishing to sign up for an X account have plenty of alternatives – Mastodon and Bluesky, for example. X has considerable form when it comes to rolling out the lawyers to fend off perceived threats or alleged abuses of its services, and is perfectly within its rights to do so. In the case of Nitter (and XCancel, which uses the project), the concern likely centered on how posts were viewed without an account, JavaScript, or ads. X also attempted to defend the Twitter and blue bird trademark and branding in a spat with tweet.app. It kept hold of Twitter (despite ditching the name after Musk's takeover), but tweet.app snapped up the word 'tweet' and the bird imagery. Who's governing your AI? A trust framework for enterprise agents and models
2026-09-15

Who's governing your AI? A trust framework for enterprise agents and models

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
SPONSORED FEATURE: DigiCert wants to hand every agent a passport, complete with an expiry date and a named human owner Experienced IT leaders know that shadow IT is a persistent problem, but rapidly evolving AI and the proliferation of agents mean the potential threat - and cost - is greater than ever. AI agents are non-deterministic, autonomous, and adaptable. They excel at solving tasks in creative ways, often to the surprise of their creators. We've seen agents write blogs that criticize project maintainers that refused their pull requests. Another one hacked a McKinsey chatbot to gain read/write access without asking for permission. And agents are getting smarter all the time. As an industry veteran, DigiCert's senior vice president of product Brian Trzupek sees an old pattern. "When the promise of the technology is so good, people are willing to throw security out the window, and they just want to get to that promise real fast." CISOs should be worried about allowing these agents into their infrastructure without strict controls, but it's happening anyway. IBM's 2026 Cost of a Data Breach report found that more organizations lacked governance to manage AI or detect shadow AI, at 68 percent compared to 63 percent last year. The number requiring IT approval to deploy AI had fallen to 38 percent from 45 percent. DigiCert is trying to solve this problem with its own approach to AI governance called AI Trust. The framework, outlined in this white paper, builds on what the company is good at: public key infrastructure, DNS, and attestation. AI Trust uses these tools to help organizations answer five AI governance questions: ·         What agents your employees are using ·         What regulated data is flowing to them ·         Whether a compromised agent can be stopped immediately ·         Whether an incident can be reconstructed with a tamper-evident trail Almost every enterprise fails at the first hurdle, warns Trzupek. Developers build agents or buy them from vendors and deploy them internally without asking. Users might also spawn agents from inside tools like Claude Desktop or OpenAI Codex that will then create sub-agents. "Those sub-agents don't assume the same rights and responsibilities and authorization as the parent agent," he says. "So they try to delegate tasks that can be wholly controlled." Most companies haven't developed the tools to keep track of all these different agent types yet. The first step is to identify them. This is where many organizations make their first mistake by bolting agents onto the human identity and access management (IAM) stack they already have. The idea is that if you give an agent a service account and a long-lived API key you can treat them like a super-fast employee. That's impractical. "IAM was built for a human sitting at a keyboard who can tap 'approve' on their phone," Trzupek says. "An agent can't do that. So you fall back to a static API key that never expires and has way more scope than it needs, and now you've undone everything zero trust was supposed to give you. It's the exact credential we've spent a decade telling people to get rid of." Industry bodies have started converging on a different answer. IDC now recommends treating agent identity as a workload identity problem rather than an extension of human IAM, aligning with the IETF's Workload Identity Management and Security Extensions (WIMSE) and NIST's Cybersecurity Framework version 2.0. They can then frame agents as governed workloads requiring runtime attestation and short-lived credentials . This idea also pushes teams toward the Service Profile Identity (SPIFFE) and its SPIFFE Registration Endpoint (SPIRE). This is an open workload identity standard already deployed inside many hyperscaler-hosted Kubernetes estates, and they're part of DigiCert's AI strategy. Inventory and identity might get you visibility, but you still need somewhere to enforce policy. DigiCert has strong opinions about where, tied to its history managing DNS integrity. No matter whether an agent is resolving an API endpoint or connecting to an MCP server, it has to query DNS first. So why not make that a core verification point? DigiCert proposes a solution that looks a lot like the DMARC standard used for email. An organization would publish an agent policy record in DNS that declares several things: ·         Its authorized agent identities ·         The certificate authority that issued their credentials ·         The scopes they're allowed to act within A gateway can then query that record to verify whether an inbound agent is legit, and terminate the session if the check fails . And if an agent contacts an unauthorized domain mid-execution, DNS can block the query and the MCP gateway kills the session. IDC likes this idea but warns that scale is an issue. As the number of agents grows, DNS records might not keep up, and stale records might become a loophole. Overly permissive scope declarations are also still a potential problem. "The scale problem is real, but it's the same problem DNS has solved a hundred times before," Trzupek responds. "You automate the lifecycle, you tie the record to the certificate issuance, and when the cert expires the record goes with it." And operators writing wildcard scopes because they're in a hurry is a discipline problem, not an architecture problem, he adds. Agents built in-house live alongside third-party agents like Microsoft Copilot, Salesforce Agentforce and ServiceNow, and the control planes for the two categories are different. DigiCert's answer is a single SPIRE server anchored to a DigiCert CA for identity, with policy enforced centrally in an Open Policy Agent engine, and a unified kill switch that operates across both categories. The AI Agent Passport is the artifact that ties the identity to the authorization. It's a cryptographically protected record of approved systems and permitted operations. Each 'passport' also contains things like data sensitivity classifications and expiration state, along with an accountable human owner. Trzupek says the field that generates the most pushback in the design is policy itself, because customers typically have a complex web of policies already in GCP or AWS. "Trying to replace those or displace them is a fool's errand," he says. So the passport can hold pointers to those engines rather than replacing them. Governing agents is only half of the challenge. The models themselves are strategic assets, and they're subject to integrity and provenance controls too. That means encryption and cryptographic signing of model artifacts, Open Container Initiative-compliant packaging with tools like the Sigstore code signing initiative, and a cryptographically verifiable Model bill of materials describing weights, datasets and dependencies . It also means governing models at runtime, not just securing the supply chain. DigiCert's AI Trust framework advocates trusted hardware execution to help solve that problem. A model running inside a trusted execution environment on Intel TDX or AMD SEV-SNP stays encrypted in memory, isolated from the host OS. DigiCert operates a confidential computing attestation service that follows the IETF Remote ATtestation Procedures (RATS) architecture. The execution environment is the attester and DigiCert is the verifier. Downstream systems are the relying parties. This approach moves attestation from a neutral third party rather than the cloud operator running the workload, which serves regulated buyers. Hyperscalers shouldn't attest to their own integrity. Those regulated buyers face some heavy governance conversations. In healthcare, the question is whether the AI model cleared through an FDA 510(k) pathway is the exact algorithm running in clinical deployment. Cryptographic attestation lets these companies prove model integrity before every inference. That addresses the FDA's 2023 cybersecurity guidance on software integrity verification and SBOM enforcement. But while some regulated verticals have specific needs, AI governance is a cross-sector problem. Any organization storing customer data an agent can access or exfiltrate needs these controls. This is agentic AI's moment, so companies are at a pivot point, and they've been here before. Many of them spent the last 25 years following a cybersecurity antipattern: move fast to grab an opportunity with a significant new technology development, and call in the security team later to clean up the loose ends. It hasn't gone well. With many organizations at the beginning of their agentic AI journey, they now have an opportunity to break that habit and do things right from the beginning using verifiable controls. As frontier model prices climb and AI crops up as a noticeable cost on enterprise budgets, this will become an increasingly visible choice, concludes Trzupek. Companies will hold people more accountable for their use of agentic technology. Wouldn't it be nice to have the controls in place and be ahead of the game for once? CenterPoint Energy confirms intruder helped themselves to customer information
2026-09-15

CenterPoint Energy confirms intruder helped themselves to customer information

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Forum post claims 7.49 million files up for grabs as Texas utility investigates breach Texas utility CenterPoint Energy has confirmed that an attacker broke in and stole customer information through one of its internet-facing systems. The company disclosed the breach in a Form 8-K filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission after a post on a cybercrime forum claimed to offer its customer data. Houston-based CenterPoint, which serves around 7 million customers, said its electricity and gas services remain operational and undisrupted. "While the investigation remains ongoing, the company has determined that an unauthorized third party obtained personal information relating to a portion of the company's customers through one of the company's external-facing systems," the filing said. "The company is continuing to work with third-party experts to determine the scope of customers and personal information affected by the incident and intends to notify affected customers and regulatory authorities as required by applicable law. "The company reported the matter to law enforcement authorities and has notified certain regulatory authorities of the issue." The person claiming responsibility said they extracted 7.49 million of CenterPoint's files from a poorly secured API. Among the data allegedly available to download are customer names and contact details, billing data, move-in dates, driver's license information, and the last four digits of Social Security numbers. The Register has not independently verified the contents of the alleged data dump. When we asked CenterPoint for comment on the veracity of the claims, it told us: "Our filing speaks for itself." CenterPoint said it does not believe the incident is reasonably likely to materially affect its financial condition or results of operations. For operators of critical infrastructure, cyberattacks can lead to far worse outcomes. Recently, disruptive attacks have been recorded at facilities in Poland and the UK, and more than 100 US water systems were affected by attacks in July. September's Windows 11 patch needs an emergency patch of its own

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Microsoft fixes RDP and Hyper-V fallout, but some USB audio stays silent Microsoft has released an out-of-band update for Windows 11 24H2 and 25H2 after September's record-breaking Patch Tuesday addressed almost 1,000 vulnerabilities across the company's products – and introduced several new problems. Those problems are related to USB Audio Class 1.0 devices, Remote Desktop Services, and host folders shared with certain Hyper-V-based Linux virtual machines via Plan9. While the Hyper-V and Remote Desktop Services issues are fixed, there is still work to do on USB audio. The update restores multichannel playback on USB Audio Class 1.0 devices that continued to work in stereo, but other affected devices may remain silent, have unresponsive volume controls, or display "This device cannot start (Code 10)" in Device Manager. Owners of devices still giving them the silent treatment were told: "Microsoft is working on a resolution and will update this documentation when more information is available." The cumulative update also includes protections for CVE-2026-62721, an elevation-of-privilege vulnerability in the Windows User-Mode Power Service. Separate September security updates for Excel also broke that rarely used function, Paste, on some installations. Microsoft has yet to issue a fix. Readers were impressively supportive of Microsoft's efforts to ensure its patches don't disrupt workflows. Paratiritis noted: "The issue with all these versions of Excel is understandable, considering such changes require testing, either manual or automated, which cannot be taken for granted for such cryptic functionality as Paste." How The Oregon Trail became a generational icon
2026-09-15

How The Oregon Trail became a generational icon

The Verge | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
On Version History: The game’s designer tells us how the brutal edutainment adventure came together. On Version History: The game’s designer tells us how the brutal edutainment adventure came together. Whether you played it in the back of your classroom or on your computer at home, if you were a kid in the last 30 years you almost certainly grew up playing The Oregon Trail. On the season 5 premiere of Version History, our fearless wagon leader David Pierce is joined by gaming journalist Chris Grant and Philip Bouchard, the designer and team leader of the game’s most iconic version. Vintage Violence is an absurdist crime thriller for phone addicts

Vintage Violence is an absurdist crime thriller for phone addicts

The Verge | 2026-09-15

Visit Original Website
Reviews from TIFF 2026: Vintage Violence, Ken Russell’s The Devils in 4K, and Gentle Monster. Reviews from TIFF 2026: Vintage Violence, Ken Russell’s The Devils in 4K, and Gentle Monster. There are a lot of movies that have tried to seamlessly integrate modern tech — text messages, livestreams, etc. — into their storytelling. Some of the most novel attempts have been in the genre space, like the Twitch-style horror flick Deadstream. But the most chaotic to date might just be Vintage Violence, an absurd crime thriller with lots of blood, jokes, and screentime. I was able to catch Vintage Violence, the latest from director Eugene Kotlyarenko, on my fifth day at the Toronto International Film Festival. Kotlyarenko has previously explored our hyper-connected culture in 2020’s Spree, a movie in which Stranger Things’ Joe Keery plays a rideshare driver who starts murdering passengers while livestreaming the crimes. But Vintage Violence takes things a step further by trying to replicate what it’s like to constantly be on your phone as the movie plays out. It’s a lot to take in, but I really enjoyed it. Unfortunately there isn’t a trailer yet to give you a better sense of how things work onscreen, but I’ll make sure to share it when they do release one. Vintage Violence was one of three films I took in on day 5 of TIFF 2026; the others were a 4K restoration of a cult classic and a devastating drama that’s on its way to Netflix. Here are my thoughts on all three. From the premise alone you can tell this movie is ridiculous. Carter (Cole Sprouse), a disgraced MrBeast-style influencer, finds a cache of vintage Levi’s while livestreaming, and one of his fans, Izumi (Kiko Mizuhara), suggests he comes to Tokyo to sell the denim for some big cash. When he gets there, just about everyone is running some kind of scam — pretty quickly the yakuza get involved, including an enforcer with a prosthetic middle finger — and things play out like a toned-down, but still brutal and bloody, Takashi Miike film. But what really elevates Vintage Violence is the way it constantly displays what characters are doing on their phones right alongside the main film. That means everything from sending text messages to livestreaming to listening to whale sounds to relax. It can get chaotic and a challenge to follow — at one point I was trying to read the chat on a Kick stream while an action scene played out beside it — but that’s also kind of the point. The Devils is one of those cult classics I’ve always meant to watch but never got around to. But this new 4K theatrical rerelease was the perfect excuse. First released in 1971, The Devils is a surreal and disturbing account of a witch trial in a small, independent town in 17th-century France. Run by an impossibly handsome priest (Oliver Reed) who openly defies a powerful cardinal, the town soon descends into pure hysteria following reports of demonic possession, which the church seizes on in order to oust the priest. This version is not only a remaster but also a director’s cut — it’s rated NC-17 — and while I can’t speak to what’s new since I hadn’t seen it before, I will say the film rarely shows its age, with enthralling and disturbing sequences merged with a poignant story about the dangers of mixing politics and religion. And in 4K the stark architecture and uncomfortable violence really pop on the big screen. In theaters on October 30th; streaming on Netflix on November 18th Marie Kreutzer’s latest feature is a difficult watch. It follows a young family that is torn apart when the police arrive at their country home one day to seize the father’s digital devices as part of a CSAM investigation. The story is primarily centered on the mother, Lucy (Léa Seydoux), a pianist who struggles with accepting a truth that threatens to upend her entire world. Gentle Monster does a remarkable job of slowly taking you through Lucy’s complicated, messy, and constantly changing understanding of the situation, but what really makes it work is Seydoux’s raw and unnerving performance, which oscillates between wishing the problem would go away to doing anything she can to protect her son. It’s a great film, but one that’s certain to leave you unsettled. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.

The monochrome Light phones are adding access to rideshare services while still minimizing feeds and notifications. If you buy something from a Verge link, Vox Media may earn a commission. More than a decade after its Kickstarter proposed a “cell phone designed to be used as little as possible,” Light is adding a direct connection to Uber and Lyft with a new Rideshare tool. For owners of either the Light Phone III or the upcoming Light Flip, they can use it to find pick up / drop off locations, see fares and ETA, and select different trip options or saved locations they frequently visit. They can use it to book the ride, and then see information about the driver and ETA, and call the driver if necessary. It’s an early preview of the LightOS “tool library” SDK that will allow developers to expand the phones’ features while still promising to minimize feeds and notifications. A blog post from Light says that “We’ll open up user submissions for the Tool Library by the end of this month, and look forward to having those available to everyone a few weeks after that!” The rideshare integration works via a partnership with Tremp, which already operates an SMS-based way to order rideshares, and users set up access via Light’s dashboard website, just like other LightOS tools. It’s not free to use, however, as Tremp charges a 10 percent service fee with a $2 minimum, but it will waive that fee for the first five rides within 30 days of creating an account. The pictures show all the necessary features operating, although we will have to see it in operation to find out if there are any hang-ups. The expansion of app-like features on a minimalist smartphone might seem counterintuitive, but Light cofounder Kaiwei Tang has addressed that previously, saying, “I’m not trying to design vintage phones… I want to design all this modern technology, from the ground up, and eliminate all the bullshit.” That’s why adding one feature users absolutely need access to — whether it’s a way to call for a ride, access to Spotify playlists, or something else — might be a way to keep them from swapping back to another device with more algorithms and alerts that constantly pop up. Fujifilm announced a follow-up to its tiny and quirky Instax Pal camera that debuted three years ago bundled with an instant printer. While the original Pal was a palm-sized camera with a round design that lacked a screen or viewfinder, the new Instax Pal 2 includes both in a design that looks like a miniaturized analog film camera. It’s also reminiscent of trendy pocket-friendly cameras like the Kodak Charmera, but with specs that may actually produce decent shots. The Instax Pal 2 will launch in the US later this month for $169.95, according to PetaPixel, and uses a ⅓.06-inch sensor paired with an f/2.2 28mm lens to capture images up to 10-megapixels in size. That’s a big step up over the Kodak Charmera’s 1.6-megapixel sensor. The Pal 2 has an optical viewfinder and a 1.3-inch LCD screen on top for framing shots and is equipped with autofocus capabilities including optional face detection plus adjustable ISO, shutter speed, and exposure compensation. Atop the Instax Pal 2 you’ll find a shutter button, a joystick, and a functional lever similar to what old film cameras used to advance to the next exposure. The camera pairs with Fujifilm’s mobile app where you can select and download image effects to the Pal 2 or upload images to your phone for printing using a wireless Bluetooth connection or a slide-out USB-C port. The camera also features a flash, a tripod mount, a self-timer, and a rechargeable battery good for up to three hours of use, Fujifilm claims. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. DIY home security company SimpliSafe is bringing its AI-powered proactive security feature to the front door. The new SimpliSafe Video Doorbell Series 2 launches today for $199.99 and works with the company’s Active Guard Outdoor Protection (starting at $49.99 a month). This combines AI analysis with live agents to detect potential threats and respond proactively. When the camera detects suspicious activity — using a combination of on-device AI, cloud-based computer vision, and facial recognition — a SimpliSafe monitoring agent can drop in on the camera to “see, speak to, and attempt to deter potential intruders and package thieves,” according to the company. The SimpliSafe Video Doorbell Series 2 is a wired doorbell with 2K resolution, a 10x zoom, a 150-degree head-to-toe view, two-way audio, and a built-in siren. It has smart alerts for people and packages, up to five seconds of pre-roll video, and supports dual-band Wi-Fi. The doorbell requires a SimpliSafe base station and keypad and works with any monitored SimpliSafe plan. It can also be used with a self-monitored plan with video recording. Active Guard was launched in 2023 on SimpliSafe’s indoor camera, arrived on outdoor cameras in 2024, and is now available on the company’s newest doorbell camera. The feature is opt in, and the agent can only view live footage when the system is armed and has been triggered. Active Guard requires a Pro or Plus SimpliSafe monitoring plan starting at $49.99 a month. In addition to potentially deterring intruders, SimpliSafe says the system can provide emergency services with more accurate information. The company has published a privacy page detailing how the system works, including that the footage is encrypted (at rest and in transit, not end-to-end) and that it “won’t share camera footage with law enforcement unless required by law.” Color-coded LED status lights on the doorbell indicate when a monitoring agent (amber) or homeowner (blue) is speaking, and you can create a library of familiar faces so the monitoring agent doesn’t scare the bejeezus out of your dog walker. Ring offers a similar proactive security feature called Virtual Guard that works with its Ring Alarm system for $99 a month. Having a human be able to see and verify whether there’s any actual danger at your home and potentially act on it has value, especially if you live in an area where false alarms incur fines, but for most people this feels like overkill.
